Convatec · CTEC-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 16 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Convatec

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 48,935 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Convatec's external environment is stable on demand and adverse on price and cost. Demand is non-discretionary and visible at 4 to 5 percent, competition is rational, and the feared disruptions are being disproved; but the US payer has already destroyed one product line, is scheduled to act on the company's most profitable channel in 2028, European payers start cutting in 2027, and the input-cost hedge rolls off in 2027 into rising polymer and freight costs. The external forces therefore contribute nothing to the guided acceleration and threaten roughly 50 to 150 basis points of margin and 1 to 2 points of revenue over 2027 and 2028 if they land together. The Consumer should model the external layer as market growth of 4 to 5 percent, explicit inflation and FX lines, and a competitive-bidding scenario pair for 2028, and should treat the recent re-rating of the sector as a valuation wave that can reverse, not as a change in fundamentals.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0247C-0538C-0745C-0955C-3562C-3946
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-4715th of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity eased over the last 12 to 18 months because both large rivals stumbled, and it is now set to tighten modestly through 2027 in Convatec's most important battleground, US ostomy and continence, as Coloplast's new chief executive reinvests most of his cost savings into US commercial capability and launches.

  • Why it eased: Coloplast spent a year without a permanent chief executive (May 2025 to May 2026), wrote down Kerecis by DKK 3.0bn, cut guidance repeatedly and on 3 Nov 2026 rebased its 2030 ambition to 5 to 7 percent organic growth and a mid-20s EBIT margin; Smith & Nephew cut FY2026 growth guidance to about 4 percent, its bioactives fell 8 percent in Q3 2026 and its orthopaedics business is under strategic review; Mölnlycke grew 2 percent. Convatec narrowed the organic growth gap with Coloplast by about three points between 2022 and 2026.
  • Why it tightens from here: Coloplast's "Funding the Journey" frees DKK 500m a year by 2027/28 with about 80 percent reinvested into innovation and commercial expansion, explicitly targeting the US (25 percent of its sales and "the largest value creation opportunity"); it plans more than ten launches in 2026/27 including a US-specific Luja catheter; it now guides to neutral-to-negative pricing, so there is no price umbrella. Coloplast also holds Vizient and Premier contracts, the same GPO access Convatec has just regained after a five-year drought. The HealthTrust single-source contract held by Hollister expired on 30 Sep 2026 and is being re-tendered.
  • Bottom of the market tightening steadily: group-purchasing concessions cost Convatec 27 basis points of adjusted gross margin in 2025; Medline pushes private-label urology and wound products; 80 percent of advanced wound products are judged interchangeable.
  • What is not changing: behaviour remains rational (C-28, C-29); nobody competes on list price in reimbursed chronic care; competition is through clinical evidence, rebates, portfolio tying and free product for hospital placements.

Implication for the model: do not assume price relief from competitors' weakness; assume ostomy share gains cost commercial spend (selling expense held, not cut) and allow for slower GPO-to-community conversion in 2027.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

F3827th of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: inputs were a tailwind in 2025 (inflation easing from 6.5 percent in 2023 to about 3 percent) and are neutral in 2026 because of hedging, but a headwind is forming for 2027 as the 12-month hedges roll off into higher polymer, freight and wage costs. The plausible size is 50 to 110 basis points of gross margin before mitigation, which bears directly on the 24 percent 2027 margin target.

  • Cost structure: cost of goods sold about 40 percent of revenue; within it raw materials about 45 percent (about 18 percent of revenue), overheads 30 percent, labour 25 percent; freight 5 percent and utilities 2 percent of cost of goods sold. Polymers and petrochemical inputs dominate; no single material is more than 5 percent of cost of goods sold.
  • 2026 protected: about 80 percent of raw materials hedged for FY2026; about five months of finished goods and key materials held; inflation planned at about 3 percent. Each point above 3 percent costs $2m to $3m in FY2026.
  • 2027 exposed: each point above 3 percent costs $7m to $8m in FY2027; if spot inflation doubles, $20m to $30m (about 80 to 110 basis points on about $2.7bn of revenue). Evidence it is forming: European polypropylene +45.3 percent and injection-grade polyethylene +39.1 percent year to date in H1 2026 (Morgan Stanley); the chief executive said in Aug 2026 that prevailing inflation is expected to double next year (while stating this will not derail the 24 percent 2027 margin); Coloplast reported raw materials +2 to 3 percent and double-digit freight in its Q4 and raised its inflation expectation to 3 to 4 percent, and missed its Q4 margin by 210 basis points largely on it. Brent fell below $100 on 5 Nov 2026 from about $115, which eases but does not remove the risk.
  • Contract rigidity: no automatic inflation pass-through in diabetes infusion contracts; US group-purchasing contracts run three years; reimbursement adjusts with a lag.
  • Supplier concentration: 42 suppliers are 80 percent of raw-material spend; single-source adhesives and silicones sit under joint patents and need lengthy validation to switch; nine contract manufacturers are 80 percent of outsourced assembly (the Oct 2026 ESENTA paste recall originated at a third-party site). Mitigation: dual sourcing, supplier audits (50 in FY2025), risk-monitoring software.
  • Currency on costs: plants in Mexico, Slovakia, Denmark and the Dominican Republic; a weaker dollar raises local costs against dollar revenue (a 10 percent dollar move against the Danish krone is worth $11.6m of pre-tax profit, against the euro $7.9m).

Implication for the model: hold the FY2026 gross margin flat-to-down on FY2025's 60.7 percent; in FY2027 put an explicit inflation line of $7m to $8m per point above 3 percent against the productivity savings, rather than assuming the consensus gross margin rise to about 60.4 percent.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C+458th of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro barely touches demand but bites hard on value, and hardest at the sector level through the discount rate and the sector's de-rating; at company level it bites through currency, where Convatec is unusually exposed as the only FTSE 100 medtech that reports in dollars and trades in pounds.

  • Sector level (strongest): US ten-year yields near 5 percent and a Bank of England hike to 4.00 percent priced; the medtech consumables sector's median stock sits at the 2nd percentile of its own valuation range (22 Oct 2026); the ostomy, continence and wound cohort fell 14.4 percent over twelve months before rising 13.0 percent over three. The house regime reading is risk-off beneath an index at its high, bearish with moderate conviction; the Sector and Industry rows are Neutral; the house notes that the defensive with leverage or a contested estimate de-rates first inside a defensive sector, which describes Convatec (2.3 to 2.6 times net debt to EBITDA; a contested H2 margin bridge).
  • Industry level: part of healthcare's recent strength is money leaving technology shares (Jefferies), which can reverse.
  • Company level, currency: a 1.5 point translation tailwind to FY2025 sales; about +160 basis points to FY2026 reported sales; but a 60 basis point transactional margin headwind in H1 2026 and about 40 for the year. A move in GBP/USD from about 1.34 to 1.39 cuts pence EPS by over 5 percent with nothing changed in the business; hedges run only 12 months (Smith & Nephew hedges up to 36). A 10 percent across-the-board dollar strengthening cuts translated revenue by about $97.5m and EPS by about 0.70 cents.
  • Company level, energy and inflation: via polymers (C-05).
  • Demand: insulated; hospital capital budget squeezes do not touch daily consumables.

Implication for the model: model in dollars, state a GBP/USD assumption for the pence EPS and the valuation, and run a sterling-strength sensitivity.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–55

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the material other dynamic is the sector valuation wave and the "worries clearing" story: Coloplast's reset on 3 Nov 2026 and the competitive-bidding bill have improved Convatec's relative position, and part of the 16 percent move since the memo is the market re-pricing that, not Convatec's own news. It can reverse with the sector.

  • Coloplast's reset changes the comparison: Coloplast now targets 5 to 7 percent organic growth and a mid-20s margin; Convatec targets 6 to 8 percent and 24 to 26 percent. Morgan Stanley notes the convergence threatens Coloplast's premium; Jefferies puts Coloplast at about 20 times CY2027 earnings, a 40 percent premium to Convatec against a 55 percent five-year average. The narrowing premium is a tailwind to Convatec's relative multiple.
  • The sector's "worries clearing" theme: the house sector note ranks "each worry that clears at Coloplast, Convatec, Smith & Nephew and Straumann lifts that company" among the strongest themes; at the bottom of a range a franchise's multiple can rise without an earnings upgrade.
  • Read-across risk: Coloplast and Smith & Nephew report on or near Convatec's dates; Smith & Nephew cut guidance again on 6 Nov 2026. A peer miss or a new CMS proposal hits Convatec regardless of its own numbers (it fell 10 percent intraday on an FDA letter with no operational effect, Feb 2026).
  • Rotation: part of healthcare's three-month rise is money leaving technology; that money can leave again.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

B+625th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: entry threat is low overall; the only credible types are adjacent players moving down from distribution (Medline private label) and orthogonal technology players in infusion (integrated-cannula patch pumps), both slow-acting. Standard entrants are blocked by regulation and clinical networks.

  • Adjacent (moderate): Medline's private-label urology and wound products, sold through its 69 distribution centres with next-day delivery, aimed at converting wholesale customers to its own brand. Pump makers in-sourcing sets: low, given Medtronic's contract of over twenty years and regulatory filings that name Convatec.
  • Standard (low): clinical-trial, biocompatibility and MDR burdens; nurse education networks; GPO placement as a licence to compete; scale needed (over 1 billion units a year in these triopolies).
  • Orthogonal or innovative (low to moderate): Insulet-type tubeless pumps with their own cannula (substitute for sets; Insulet hired Convatec's former research chief); Chinese makers (Cofoe) with quality improving and prices 50 to 72 percent lower, a threat in Asia and online channels, not in Convatec's markets; Indian skin-substitute makers at about $20 per square centimetre; MediWound's EscharEx (Phase III in venous leg ulcers to Dec 2027); artificial intelligence wound-measurement tools.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4622nd of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a moderate source of risk rather than a support: raw materials are concentrated among a few dozen suppliers, specialty adhesives and silicones are single-source under joint patents with real pricing power, and the cost of those inputs is rising into 2027; the company manages supply security well (inventory, dual sourcing, audits) but cannot pass costs through quickly.

  • Concentration and power: 42 suppliers are about 80 percent of raw-material spend; specialty chemical makers (Dow cited) hold pricing power; in past shortages silicone suppliers squeezed adhesive formulators; switching needs clinical validation and regulatory pre-approval (up to five years for adhesives at Coloplast).
  • Outsourcing: nine contract manufacturers are about 80 percent of outsourced assembly; the ESENTA recall (Oct 2026) came from a third-party site.
  • Mitigation: 80 percent hedge for 2026; five months of stock; dual sourcing accelerated; supplier risk-monitoring software; 50 supplier audits in FY2025; payment terms standardised at 90 days (a $10.5m working-capital timing benefit).
  • Direction: a headwind building into 2027 (C-05).
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0155C-0360C-0438C-0852C-3266C-3738C-4355
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D5522nd of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market demand is structural, steady and highly visible, and it is not changing in a way that accelerates the company. The acceleration Convatec guides for (6 to 8 percent organic from 2027) must come from share gains of roughly 1.5 to 3.5 points a year above a 4 to 5 percent market, not from the market.

  • Structural and non-discretionary. Over 90 percent of revenue is recurring consumables for incurable or long-lasting conditions (stomas, neurogenic bladder, chronic wounds, insulin and Parkinson's infusion). Market growth: chronic care 4 to 5 percent (DNB Carnegie puts the weighted underlying market at 5 to 6 percent); advanced wound care about $6bn at mid-single digit; ostomy $3.7bn to $4.0bn at mid-single digit; continence $2.4bn to $2.7bn at mid-single digit; infusion sets about $1bn at high-single digit. Ageing (over-65s from 0.8bn to 1.9bn by 2060) and diabetes prevalence underpin it. No cyclical swing exists to exploit; demand fell only low-to-mid single digits for medtech even in 2009.
  • Last six months against the prior 12 to 18: slightly softer at the edge, not at the core. The global wound market grew about one point more slowly in H1 2026; European healthcare budgets are tight (Mölnlycke +2 percent organic; Smith & Nephew's Advanced Wound Management weak; Coloplast's wound and tissue repair +1 percent in its Q4). Ostomy and continence demand held (Coloplast's Ostomy +6 percent and Continence +5 percent in Q4; Convatec Ostomy +4.3 percent and Continence +5.9 percent in H1 2026). US skin-substitute demand collapsed (the market expected to shrink from about $13bn to under $3bn), but that is a payer event, not a demand trend, and it is now out of Convatec's base.
  • The accelerating pockets are small. Subcutaneous Parkinson's therapies (AbbVie expects Vyalev above $1bn of sales in 2026; Convatec is sole set supplier to AbbVie, Supernus and Tanabe) and automated insulin delivery are real accelerators, but non-diabetes infusion is about 15 percent of a division that is 19 percent of revenue, about 3 percent of the group.
  • Visibility is unusually high. Infusion partners forecast up to a year ahead and place firm orders months ahead; ostomy patients stay with the brand chosen at hospital discharge for up to ten years; 180 Medical churn is below 1 percent.
  • Next six months: stable. The guided H2 2026 acceleration to 6 to 8 percent is company-specific (order phasing in Infusion, launches, the InnovaMatrix base) and should not be modelled as market acceleration.

Implication for the model: build revenue from market growth of 4 to 5 percent plus named share drivers by segment (C-43, C-22); treat any revenue assumption above about 7 percent as share gain that must be named.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

B606th of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the disruption forces the market feared are being disproved or are acting in Convatec's favour; the one that did real damage, US payment reform for skin substitutes, has already run its course. The live long-dated disruption to watch is tubeless patch pumps, where the company has started to participate but not yet to earn.

  • GLP-1 weight-loss drugs: being disproved for Convatec's categories. Initiation raises continuous glucose monitor use two to four times (Redburn), wound prevalence tied to ageing is stable over three years (Bernstein), and diabetes device use is neutral to positive.
  • Patch pumps (tubeless insulin delivery): strengthening as a category (about 14 percent annual growth to 2030), which threatens tubed-pump infusion sets, Convatec's core. Convatec signed its first patch-pump supply agreement in H1 2026 and a partnership with MiniMed for next-generation patch pumps, showing its cannula expertise transfers; Redburn expects tubed pumps to keep their dominant share near term. Insulet, the leading patch-pump maker with its own integrated cannula, hired Convatec's former head of research in Jun 2026. Direction: neutral now, a two-sided option from 2027.
  • Home care migration: strengthening and positive. Care moving from hospital to home favours 180 Medical and the home-services model.
  • Single-use negative pressure wound therapy and nitric oxide dressings: disruptive categories Convatec is entering (ConvaVac, FDA 510(k) Jun 2026, targeting 1 percent to 5 percent share of a category growing about 13 percent a year; ConvaNiox, a new multimodal category).
  • Payment-model disruption: the move from cost-plus to flat-rate payment (skin substitutes at $127.28 per square centimetre) destroyed a premium segment for Convatec, Coloplast and Smith & Nephew alike. It is finished for InnovaMatrix (under 1 percent of sales). Competitive bidding in 2028 is the next instance (C-04).
  • Digital ostomy sensing and artificial intelligence wound tools: long-dated and funding-constrained.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3830th of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulation is the strongest external force on the case and it is a headwind, with one dated binary event, US Medicare competitive bidding from 1 Jan 2028, to which Convatec is the most exposed of its peers because of 180 Medical. A bipartisan bill to exclude ostomy and continence for five years gives the event a two-sided skew; a new European pricing reform from Jan 2027, visible in Coloplast's disclosures, is a read-across the Convatec research has not yet absorbed.

  • Skin substitutes, done: Medicare's flat $127.28 per square centimetre rate from 1 Jan 2026 (an 85 percent price cut on InnovaMatrix) took InnovaMatrix from $99m (FY2024) to $69m (FY2025) to $2.5m in H1 2026, with $141m of impairments across FY2025 and H1 2026. The restrictive Local Coverage Determinations were withdrawn on 24 Dec 2025. Nothing further to model beyond $5m to $10m in FY2026 and near zero after.
  • Competitive bidding, Jan 2028: an expected 30 percent cut to retail (supplier) prices and about 15 percent to manufacturers' wholesale prices; contracted national suppliers reduced from over 3,000 to about 8 to 10. Exposure: 7 percent of group sales reimbursed directly by Medicare through 180 Medical, plus 2 percent through Medicare-dependent distributors. Management estimates a 1 to 2 percent group revenue headwind in the implementation year; Redburn models 1.2 percent; Morgan Stanley puts it at a 3 to 8 percent cut to 2028 consensus EPS if it proceeds and mid-single-digit upside if the bill passes. The EPS impact is larger than the revenue impact because a cut at 180 Medical falls on the retail markup with near-full drop-through. Offset: volume shifting to surviving national suppliers (a third of the market is held by non-contracted distributors). Bidder registration opens 1 Dec 2026. Consensus FY2028 revenue growth of +6.8 percent suggests most brokers carry little of it.
  • Positive US change: three new Medicare codes for hydrophilic catheters from Jan 2026 support conversion (over 60 percent of Convatec's US catheter sales are hydrophilic against about 40 percent of the market).
  • FDA: the Unomedical Reynosa warning letter (Jan 2026, complaint handling, no production restriction) is expected to be lifted by end-2027.
  • Europe: EU Medical Device Regulation certification secured (BSI, 28 Sep 2026) and a barrier to entrants. New: Coloplast states that pricing policies in France and Germany from Jan 2027 will cut prices by a low-single-digit percentage across chronic care and advanced wound dressings. Convatec's Europe is 30 percent of sales; France and Germany are a fraction of that, so the group effect is likely tens of basis points of revenue in 2027, but it is not in Convatec's guidance commentary.
  • Tariffs: much of the portfolio enters the US duty-free under the Nairobi Protocol; H1 2026 net tariff effect +$5m (refund timing); DNB Carnegie's worst case on removal is 4 percent of EBIT.

Implication for the model: carry competitive bidding explicitly in FY2028 as a scenario pair (proceeds: revenue -1 to -2 percent and EPS -3 to -8 percent; deferred: neither), and pencil in a small European price headwind in FY2027.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F5225th of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: no industry or sector driver is accelerating Convatec's demand at group level. The drivers that are accelerating are niches inside Infusion (subcutaneous Parkinson's therapy, automated insulin delivery) and a mix shift in US catheters, worth perhaps half a point to a point of group growth a year, not a demand inflection.

  • Subcutaneous drug delivery (Parkinson's): AbbVie's Vyalev, Supernus's Onapgo and a Tanabe therapy each specify Convatec's set in their regulatory filings; Morgan Stanley estimates Vyalev alone could add up to 3 points to Infusion growth; penetration of pump-delivered Parkinson's therapy is about 5 percent. Partner supply constraints (Supernus) are a named risk.
  • Automated insulin delivery: drove Infusion's 12.5 percent organic growth in FY2025; constrained historically by Convatec's own capacity; a new automated high-speed line comes online in 2027 backed by minimum volume commitments.
  • Hydrophilic catheter conversion (US): new Medicare codes from Jan 2026; this is Coloplast's top-ranked driver (worth about 1.5 percent of its revenue). Convatec is already 60 percent hydrophilic in the US, so its uplift is smaller but positive in mix.
  • Premiumisation to soft convex ostomy products: Esteem Body now; Natura Body into the two-piece soft convex segment (60 to 65 percent of the US ostomy market) in 2027.
  • Ageing and home care: steady, not accelerating.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C+668th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer behaviour supports the thesis well in ostomy, continence and infusion (sticky, recurring, forecastable) and less well in wound, where buyers choose by indication, evidence and reimbursement and switch easily; the fragility is concentrated where the "customer" is a payer or a procurement committee rather than a nurse or a patient.

  • Ostomy: nurses choose at discharge; patients stay up to ten years; European patients are shifting to higher-priced convex and concave plates; Esteem Body is capturing that shift.
  • Continence: patients prioritise service over brand and experiment with brands in Europe; hydrophilic, residue-free catheters are gaining (GentleCath Air series growing double digits in volume); 180 Medical's service (NPS 80, churn below 1 percent) holds the patient, and the patient can be moved to own brand.
  • Infusion: customers are pump makers and drug companies that forecast a year ahead and specify the set in their filings; demand is visible, but partner concentration is high (a handful of pump and drug partners) and diabetes contracts carry no inflation pass-through.
  • Wound: purchase by indication and evidence; hospital supply-chain managers increasingly override clinicians to consolidate vendors; 80 percent of products interchangeable; reimbursement availability drives choice (German expert); care moving to the home favours simple products.
  • Concentration: no customer above 10 percent of revenue.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3830th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and politicians are the decisive and least predictable actors in the value chain; on balance they hurt (price-setting by rule), with real but smaller help (catheter codes, supplier consolidation, MDR as a barrier, a bidding-delay bill). The position is fragile because a single rule can reset a category's price, as InnovaMatrix showed, and the next rule is dated to 2028.

  • CMS (hurts, then helps on volume): flat-rate skin-substitute payment (an 85 percent price cut on InnovaMatrix); audits and the WISeR prior-review programme cut outpatient wound volumes by up to 75 percent; competitive bidding for ostomy and continence from Jan 2028; but new hydrophilic catheter codes (Jan 2026), withdrawal of restrictive coverage determinations (Dec 2025), and consolidation of suppliers favouring national players.
  • Congress (may help): a bipartisan bill of 9 Oct 2026 proposes excluding ostomy and continence from bidding for five years; Morgan Stanley puts passage at mid-single-digit upside to 2028 consensus EPS. A precedent from the house library applies: a smaller-than-feared or delayed tax is relief for a repeat payer, not an all-clear.
  • FDA (neutral to slightly hurts): warning letter at Reynosa to be lifted by end-2027; clearances flowing (ConvaVac 510(k) Jun 2026; ConvaNiox de novo pending).
  • European regulators and payers: MDR raises costs but protects incumbents (BSI certification Sep 2026); France and Germany price reforms from Jan 2027 (Coloplast disclosure); German silver-dressing reimbursement pressure absorbed through launches.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5526th of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic core market is about $13bn to $14bn growing 4 to 5 percent a year, of which Convatec holds about 18 percent; with adjacent categories now being entered it is large enough to support the guided 6 to 8 percent organic growth through share gains, but not organic growth anywhere near 12 to 20 percent.

  • Core: advanced wound care about $6.0bn (narrow definition; $11.7bn to $13bn including traditional dressings) at mid-single digit; ostomy $3.7bn to $4.0bn at 5 to 6 percent; continence $2.4bn to $2.7bn at mid-single digit; infusion sets about $1bn at high single digit.
  • Adjacent, growing faster: single-use negative pressure therapy (about 13 percent a year); patch-pump sets (patch pumps about 14 percent a year to 2030); subcutaneous Parkinson's therapy (about 5 percent penetration); the nitric-oxide dressing category (new); US two-piece soft convex ostomy (60 to 65 percent of the US ostomy market, which Natura Body enters in 2027).
  • Arithmetic: a 4.5 to 5 percent market plus 1.5 to 3 points of share gain gives 6 to 8 percent; the guided segment mix (Infusion double digit, Advanced Wound Care high single digit from 2028, Ostomy and Continence mid-to-high single digit) is consistent with that.

The optical inflection will be large: group organic growth from about 1.8 percent in H1 2026 and about 3 percent for FY2026 to about 6 to 7 percent in FY2027, which matters for the narrative even though the underlying ex-InnovaMatrix rate moves only from about 5 to 6 percent to 6 to 7 percent.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D4522nd of 32

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Taking each thematic driver in turn, and then all together: how strong are the external headwinds and tailwinds, and what is the overall external picture?

Judgement: the external picture is a mild net headwind to earnings over 2027 and 2028 set against a mild, steady tailwind to demand. External forces do not add growth to the case; at best they stop subtracting. The case therefore rests on internal change (the next group), and the external variables that most need modelling are the payer, input inflation and currency.

Thematic drivers, one by one:

  • Ageing and chronic prevalence (industry, demand): steady tailwind, low strength (4 to 5 percent market growth). Reliable.
  • Home care migration (value chain): tailwind, moderate, favours 180 Medical and home services.
  • Payer cost containment (value chain, US and Europe): the strongest force, a headwind: skin substitutes done; competitive bidding 2028 (1 to 2 percent of revenue, 3 to 8 percent of EPS); France and Germany price reforms from 2027; offset by US catheter codes and supplier consolidation favouring 180 Medical.
  • Diabetes technology and subcutaneous drug delivery (industry): tailwind, moderate, concentrated in Infusion; patch pumps two-sided.
  • Input costs (value chain): neutral in 2026, a headwind forming for 2027 (50 to 110 basis points of gross margin before mitigation).
  • Competition (industry): eased, now tightening modestly as Coloplast reinvests in the US.
  • Discount rate and sector valuation wave (sector): the deepest headwind of the last two years, now turning as worries clear, but in a risk-off regime and still fragile.
  • Currency (company within sector): two-sided; sterling strength is the damaging tail.

All together: headwinds are moderate and identifiable (payer, inflation, sterling); tailwinds are steady but slow (demand) or narrow (Infusion niches). The biggest external swing for the four-year model is one dated decision, competitive bidding.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal change forces are the case. They are broad, measurable and funded: a raised mid-term growth target backed by a dated launch programme, an outlier cost programme (G&A from 13 to 6 percent of sales) with automation still half done, contracted Infusion capacity, and a systematic buyback that the leverage policy implies will continue beyond 2026 although consensus assumes it stops. Their weakness is that they are mature (the first act is in the numbers) and that the leaders who must deliver the 2026 to 2027 step are new or missing (chief executive since Nov 2025, chief financial officer from Mar 2027, research chief gone, operations lead being recruited). For the model, the Consumer should treat cost savings and buybacks as the high-confidence legs of EPS growth, launches and Infusion as the medium-confidence revenue legs, and leadership transition as the reason to put odds below certainty on the H2 2026 margin step.

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is moderate and well identified, and it has worsened in one respect since the memo: the price. The fundamental risks are mostly regulatory and dated (competitive bidding in 2028; FDA remediation to 2027) or operational and low-probability (Reynosa, recall, plant); the deceleration risk is explained and testable at tomorrow's ten-month update and the 23 Feb 2027 results; the sentiment risks (peer and payer read-across, sterling, sector rotation, a unanimous sell-side) are the ones most likely to move the shares in 18 months. At 252.8p and 13.4 times 24-month-forward earnings, a miss on the H2 2026 margin step would take the shares back towards 185p to 205p. For the model, the Consumer should carry a bear case that combines an FY2026 margin of about 22.5 percent, a 2027 margin about 100 basis points below consensus on unhedged inflation, and competitive bidding proceeding in 2028.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: standards are improving measurably on cost and operations, and these improvements are the most reliable part of the case; they are uneven on quality systems and governance, where the FDA warning letter, a recall and the free-cash-flow redefinition show that the scaffolding lags the operating engine.

  • Cost discipline (outlier): G&A from 13 percent of sales in 2019 to 7.2 percent in 2024, 6.8 percent in FY2025 and about 6 percent in H1 2026, through Convatec Business Services, which now covers finance, IT, HR, procurement, pricing and legal; a fourth centre opens in Hyderabad in late 2026; agentic AI and Copilot in production in quality, supply chain and finance in 2026. 130 basis points of commercial and G&A productivity in H1 2026.

Positives: a new chief compliance officer (Nov 2025), auditor rotated to EY for FY2026, six voluntary recalls in 2025 with no Class 1.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

–52

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: moderately concentrated. The forecast differs from the last four to six years in three ways that matter for EPS (faster revenue, a slower margin climb, a falling share count), and each of the three leans on one driver: Infusion volume, the operating-cost and automation bridge against inflation, and the 2.0 times leverage policy. A fourth difference is one dated binary, competitive bidding in 2028. Two things make this tolerable: the three legs are largely independent of each other, and the most concentrated one, Infusion, is also the most visible. Two things make it less comfortable: today's acceleration was narrower than the headline suggests, and one physical site, the Reynosa infusion plant, sits under two of the three legs at once.

Difference from history Size What generates it How concentrated Visibility and control
Faster revenue growth 6.1 percent a year FY2025 to FY2030 (6.5 percent from FY2026) against 4.9 percent (FY2019 to FY2025) and 5.6 percent (FY2022 to FY2025) Infusion at 10 to 13 percent; Continence and 180 Medical; launches in ostomy and continence Concentrated: in the ten months, Infusion (a fifth of revenue) supplied about 40 percent of growth and Continence about a quarter High: partners forecast 12 months ahead and order three to six months ahead under minimum volume commitments; but few customers and two plants
Slower margin climb About 58 basis points a year (22.3 to 25.2 percent by FY2030) against about 115 (2021 to 2025) One bridge: operating-cost leverage and automation against inflation, depreciation and reinvestment Concentrated in one bridge with one external variable (each point of inflation above 3 percent costs $7m to $8m in 2027) Costs in management's control; inflation not; the 2027 hedge cover undisclosed
Falling share count About 3.8 percent a year against a rising count to FY2024 The 2.0 times leverage policy One driver Wholly in management's control; the risks are a choice of acquisitions instead, and a new chief financial officer
A step down in FY2028 About 1 percent of revenue, 50 basis points of margin, 3.5 percent of EPS Medicare competitive bidding One binary decision External; largely resolved by late summer 2027
Free cash flow more than doubling About $205m (FY2026) to about $450m (FY2030) Capital expenditure falling from 9 to about 5.6 percent of sales One driver Guided (5 to 7 percent of sales from 2028)

How the EPS growth to FY2027 divides. EPS rises about 31 percent from 17.6 cents (FY2025) to 23.0 cents (FY2027). About 40 percent of that comes from revenue, a little under 30 percent from margin and a little under 30 percent from the share count; interest and tax add almost nothing. No single leg carries the forecast, and a failure in one does not cause a failure in another: cost savings do not depend on launches, and buybacks depend only on EBITDA and cash.

Why today's acceleration is narrower than it looks. On my arithmetic, of about $72m of extra revenue excluding InnovaMatrix in July to October, Infusion supplied about half, at a growth rate of about 20 percent that reflects orders phased out of the first half and better output from existing lines; Continence supplied about a fifth. Ostomy and Advanced Wound Care, 59 percent of revenue, grew at the same rates as in the first half. The acceleration confirms the second set's view that Infusion and launches are the load-bearing sources of growth. It does not show that the group as a whole has stepped up, and the 6 to 8 percent target still needs either Infusion to keep doing most of the work or one of the two large segments to move.

The forecast is more concentrated than the record that makes it credible. The 2019 to 2025 improvement was diffuse: general and administrative costs, plant closures, exits of low-margin lines, a rebuilt research budget, a pricing centre, and every segment growing 4 to 7 percent. The next four years lean on fewer things. The Consumer should not let the breadth of the past record stand in for breadth in the forecast.

The common point of failure. Infusion sets for part of the range come from one plant, Reynosa, which is under an FDA warning letter whose remediation runs through 2027. An escalation there would hit the revenue leg (Infusion growth), the margin leg (Infusion mix is 50 basis points of the H2 2026 bridge and 15 to 20 basis points a year afterwards) and the narrative together. It is a low-probability event (D-04), but it is where the concentration is real rather than apparent. The two other shared variables are milder: inflation touches margin and, through cash, buyback capacity; the dollar touches every translated line.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C6511th of 32

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy changed in emphasis, not in kind: FISBE (fix, then build) became Accelerate (April 2026), which raises the growth target and keeps the margin target, and it is well articulated and well resourced financially. Its distinctive choice is to put growth capital into contracted Infusion capacity and to return surplus cash through buybacks rather than acquisitions.

  • Articulated: four pillars (customer growth, technology, execution excellence, performance culture); three innovation waves (eight launches in 2022 to 2025, eight more in 2026 to 2028, with launch cycle times about 30 percent shorter); segment-level targets (Infusion double digit; Advanced Wound Care high single digit from 2028; Ostomy and Continence mid-to-high single digit from 2027); group 6 to 8 percent organic, 24 to 26 percent margin by 2027, double-digit EPS growth.

  • Organisational change: a move from decentralised, geography-led units to central control of technology, processes and systems; a former research director warns this causes short-term disruption and loss of people.
  • Capital allocation: organic reinvestment first, progressive dividend (35 to 45 percent payout), buybacks as the default use of surplus cash at 2.0 times leverage, bolt-on M&A only at high hurdles.
  • Market view: Jefferies calls it "a natural evolution rather than a structural reset"; the 6 percent share-price bounce after the Capital Markets Day was given back.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F3530th of 32

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms?

Judgement: Convatec is not raising prices in real terms; pricing has moved from about -1 percent a year to slightly positive (+30 basis points) in nominal terms, well below cost inflation of about 3 percent. Price gains come through mix (premium launches), not through like-for-like price, and management does not signal real price rises; it says the opposite.

  • Evidence: the Strategic Pricing Centre of Excellence delivered +30 basis points of price in FY2025 and in H1 2026 (an earlier +60 basis point gain is also cited), against a history of about 1 percent annual price erosion in chronic care.
  • Management language: inflation is to be mitigated "through pricing and productivity improvements without relying on general price increases".
  • Constraints: reimbursed categories (US Medicare fee schedules; European national tariffs); three-year group-purchasing contracts; no indexation in diabetes infusion contracts; Coloplast now guides neutral-to-negative pricing; European reforms from 2027; competitive bidding from 2028.
  • Latent: premium-priced new products (ConvaNiox at £40; soft convex systems carry a 20 to 30 percent premium at Coloplast; Luja a 5 to 10 percent premium; hydrophilic catheters coded separately from 2026) give mix-driven price of perhaps 1 to 2 percent a year, which is how Coloplast earns its price.

Implication for the model: like-for-like price of about zero, mix of plus 1 to 2 points inside the volume line, and explicit negative price events in 2027 (Europe) and 2028 (US).

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns?

Judgement: shareholder returns have been stepped up materially and systematically, and the policy implies they continue: with leverage held at 2.0 times and no ambition to go below it, rising EBITDA and normalising capital expenditure create capacity for buybacks of roughly $250m to $350m a year from 2027 on our arithmetic, while consensus assumes a flat share count after 2026.

  • Done: no buybacks 2021 to 2023; $300m bought back Aug to Dec 2025 (94.9m shares; about 4.5 percent accretive to FY2025 EPS on Morgan Stanley's arithmetic); $200m announced 4 Aug 2026 to complete by 31 Dec 2026.
  • Dividend: FY2025 7.244 cents (+13 percent, 40 percent payout); interim 2026 2.166 cents (+15.4 percent); policy 35 to 45 percent payout.
  • Balance sheet use: net debt excluding leases $1,330m at end-2025 (2.0 times), $1.53bn at H1 2026 (2.3 times, seasonal plus buyback), back to about 2.0 times by year-end; Jefferies reports no ambition to fall below 2.0 times; Morgan Stanley calls buybacks the clear default use of excess cash; the chief financial officer says buybacks depend on headroom, not on the share price.
  • Our capacity arithmetic (for the Consumer to test): each year EBITDA grows about $70m to $75m (consensus), which at 2.0 times adds about $140m to $150m of debt capacity; free cash flow after dividends is about $150m to $230m in FY2027 to FY2028 (consensus free cash flow $338m to $403m less dividends of about $175m to $195m). Together, about $250m to $350m a year, or 4 to 5 percent of the market value.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–66

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: three mechanical positives arrive within twelve months that need no heroics: the end of the 2008 spin-out amortisation (reported earnings converge on adjusted), the InnovaMatrix base effect, and a new contracted Infusion capacity line; a fourth, a competitive-bidding delay, is plausible and binary.

  • Amortisation roll-off: about $93m to $95m a year of amortisation of intangibles from the 2008 Bristol Myers Squibb spin-out ends in Dec 2026 (one source says Jul 2026). Adjusted EPS is unaffected; reported EPS (8.6 cents basic in FY2025) rises sharply towards adjusted, which improves reported-earnings screens, FactSet-based multiples and the earnings-quality narrative.
  • InnovaMatrix base effect: adds about 2.5 to 3 points to group organic growth in FY2027 against FY2026 and removes a 140 basis point margin drag seen in H1 2026.
  • Infusion capacity: a new automated high-speed line due in 2027, backed by minimum volume commitments, releases the capacity constraint an Infusion research lead says has historically held growth below demand.
  • Plausible: passage of the bipartisan bill deferring competitive bidding (mid-single-digit upside to 2028 consensus EPS per Morgan Stanley); a further buyback announced with the FY2026 results; slow conversion of the Vizient and Premier wins into community patients who stay up to ten years; Esteem Body and Natura Body share gains.

Judgement: there is no controlling actor; with Novo Holdings gone and no activist, strategy sits entirely with management and a largely independent board, so no outside force will impose change, for better or worse. The only shareholder signal is on pay, not strategy.

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C6411th of 32

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive internal changes are broad (five of the twelve change forces are strong: raised mid-term growth target, standards, new products, shareholder returns, strategy) and financially well resourced, but they are the second act of a programme running since 2019, so much of their effect is already in the numbers; the resource that is short is leadership bandwidth, not money. They are strong enough to carry earnings growth of about 13 to 15 percent a year to 2027, not strong enough to make the case new.

  • Strength: the outputs show the engine works (margin +460 basis points since 2021; five consecutive years of organic growth above 5 percent; EPS +16 percent in FY2025).
  • Breadth: cost, product, capital return and commercial levers all move at once and are largely independent (cost savings do not depend on launch success), which lowers joint failure risk.
  • Resourcing: capital expenditure, research and development and inventory buffers are funded; the chief financial officer, research and operations seats are the gap until 2027.
  • Maturity: the second act sits at the operating-metric and first financial-statement layers; consensus has moved only 3 to 4 percent in twelve months.

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5815th of 32

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5815th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry is one of the better structures in European healthcare for about 70 percent of Convatec's revenue: concentrated, rational, sticky, high-barrier and growing 4 to 5 percent, with competitors who protect margins and do not fight on price. Its weaknesses for Convatec are position and payer. Position: Convatec leads only in infusion sets and US continence delivery and is the number three challenger in ostomy and a number four in fragmented wound care, so its guided acceleration depends on winning discharges and evaluations from a Coloplast that has just reset and chosen to reinvest in the US. Payer: the single most powerful actor in the chain has already destroyed one Convatec line by rule and is scheduled to cut the price in its best channel in 2028, with a delay bill pending. History says companies in this sector outperform when earnings growth rises through the group's valuation wave and the company's own multiple re-rates as worries clear; Convatec has the earnings and a cleared worry, but its own decade shows the multiple can fall faster than the earnings rise. For the model: stable gross margin and low churn are justified by structure; share gains should be modelled product by product (Esteem and Natura Body, ConvaFoam, GentleCath Air, Infusion contracts); and the payer belongs in explicit dated scenarios.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C+629th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is a net help because of 180 Medical, a captive, double-margin US channel with 40 percent share, and because Convatec has regained US ostomy GPO access; but the same captive channel is the most exposed asset to the 2028 payer change, and the wholesale half of the business is exposed to distributors that also sell their own brands.

  • Captive channel: 180 Medical generates about 90 percent of continence divisional sales directly; Convatec earns the manufacturing margin (about 60 to 65 percent gross) and the retail margin (about 25 percent gross, 10 to 15 percent EBIT). Its billing across about 1,500 health plans and national scale are hard to replicate. Competitive bidding cuts its retail price by about 30 percent in 2028 but may hand it orphaned volume.
  • Hospital access: first ostomy GPO contracts in five years with Captis/Vizient (Nov 2025) and Premier AscenDrive (Feb 2026), opening about 40,000 US procedures a year (about a third of the market). Conversion to community share is slow (DNB Carnegie): it builds over years as discharged patients accumulate.
  • Wholesale: about 85 percent of ostomy and 15 percent of continence sales go through third parties (McKesson, Cardinal, Medline in the US; about 80 distributors in Europe and the rest of the world, with low visibility of end users). Medline converts customers to its own brand; DME suppliers can substitute brands where prescriptions name only a generic code.
  • Europe: manufacturers cannot sell directly to patients in many markets; Convatec has expanded owned home-care distribution (Livramedom in France).
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C-5614th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors' troubles have helped Convatec over the last two years (it gained relative growth while Coloplast and Smith & Nephew cut guidance), but that help is fading: Coloplast has reset its base and is reinvesting in the US, and Convatec's position in US ostomy, its main share-gain battleground, is fragile against a reinvesting Coloplast and a dominant Hollister.

  • Coloplast: Q4 2025/26 organic growth 5 percent; adjusted EBIT margin 24.8 percent (against 26.9 percent consensus); new 2030 ambition 5 to 7 percent growth, mid-20s margin; Kerecis pivoted to inpatient (about 85 percent of US biologics revenue); Luja US launch; SenSura Mio Convex at 20 to 30 percent premiums; Heylo digital ostomy (31 percent fewer leaks in a pivotal study).
  • Hollister: 40 to 50 percent of US ostomy; the HealthTrust single-source contract it held (about 15 percent of US acute ostomy) expired 30 Sep 2026 and is being re-tendered, a potential opening for Convatec and Coloplast.
  • Smith & Nephew: weak in wound (bioactives -8 percent in Q3 2026; another guidance cut; orthopaedics strategic review); a share donor in wound.
  • Solventum, Mölnlycke, BD, Wellspect: Solventum leads wound; Mölnlycke premium foam at 20 to 25 percent premiums on Safetac; BD's PureWick drives female external continence; Wellspect about 7 percent of continence.
  • Fragility: moderate. Ostomy patients stay where they start, so the battle is at discharge, and Coloplast has more acute share (about 25 percent of US hospital accounts) and now more money to spend there.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F3830th of 32full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D5023rd of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the main disruption in this value chain is payment-model disruption by public payers, and it acts hardest on distributors and on manufacturers of premium-priced reimbursed products; Convatec is exposed on both counts through 180 Medical and its former biologics line, but its integrated position also makes it a likely volume winner when the distributor layer is forced to consolidate. The net leaves its position moderately fragile in price and moderately strong in share.

  • On payers and regulators: shifting from cost-plus (average selling price plus 6 percent) to flat rates and competitive bids; this is the disruption.
  • On distributors: competitive bidding cuts US DME suppliers from over 3,000 to about 8 to 10 national contractors; small independents exit; survivors gain volume at lower prices; in China, volume-based procurement disintermediates high-markup distributors.
  • On manufacturers: premium-priced reimbursed lines (biologics) lose their economics; integrated makers swap rivals' products for own brands in their channels.
  • On demand: care moving to the home; GLP-1 drugs neutral to positive; Parkinson's therapy moving to subcutaneous pumps (positive for Convatec).
  • On competition: private label and low-cost Asian makers in commoditised segments; patch pumps in infusion.
  • On suppliers: polymer and petrochemical price spikes tied to energy; specialty adhesive makers holding IP leverage; tariffs.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C6811th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: about 70 percent of Convatec's revenue (ostomy, continence and infusion) sits in highly concentrated, steep oligopolies; the remaining 30 percent (advanced wound care) sits in a fragmented, flatter market where products are largely interchangeable and procurement decides.

  • Ostomy: top three hold 76 to 81 percent; the tail (B. Braun, Salts, Welland, regional Asian makers such as Cofoe) is small, so the top five hold far more than five times numbers six to ten.
  • Continence: top three hold about 69 percent; steep.
  • Infusion sets: near monopoly (about two-thirds Convatec).
  • Advanced wound care: the top four hold about 46 percent and the top five about 51 to 56 percent (with Coloplast at 4 to 10 percent); traditional dressings are about 62 percent of an $11.7bn market; the curve is flatter (top five perhaps two to three times numbers six to ten) because regional treatment protocols and procurement vary.
  • US home distribution: fragmented today (over 3,000 Medicare suppliers), about to be forced into a concentrated structure (about 8 to 10 national contract holders) by competitive bidding, with 180 Medical among the largest.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C+668th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares are very stable in ostomy, continence and infusion because the clinician chooses at discharge and the patient rarely switches; shifts take years and come from GPO access and product cycles, not price. New entrants have taken almost nothing in the West in five years; the only entrants that gained (biologic skin-substitute makers) were cut back by the payer.

  • Stickiness: over 90 percent of chronic patients stay with the brand chosen in hospital; ostomy loyalty up to ten years in the community (10 to 30 years at Coloplast); continence users are more service-loyal than brand-loyal (180 Medical churn below 1 percent, average tenure three to five years).
  • Slow shifts that did happen: Convatec's US ostomy share fell from about 40 to about 20 percent over 15 years after GPO losses; Coloplast gained about 7 points of US acute share since 2019; Convatec narrowed the organic growth gap to Coloplast by about 3 points from 2022 to 2026 and Esteem Body reached about 15 percent of the US soft convex segment within two years.
  • New entrants: biologics makers (Kerecis, Organogenesis, Triad) took share in US wound in 2019 to 2024 and were then crushed by flat-rate payment (US biologics expected to shrink from about $13bn to under $3bn); Medline's private label grows in wholesale and wound; Chinese locals (Cofoe, priced 50 to 65 percent below) gain in China, where Convatec has under 2 percent of sales.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5822nd of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does share consolidate towards the largest or most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them?

Judgement: in ostomy and continence the gravitational pull has historically been towards Coloplast, the largest and best-resourced; that pull has paused, and recent evidence shows the most advantaged product wins regardless of maker. Convatec is the consolidator in infusion sets and is positioned to be among the winners of the forced consolidation of US home distribution, but it is a challenger, not the beneficiary of the pull, in ostomy and wound.

  • Towards the largest: Coloplast's scale in European wholesale, nurse engagement and R&D pulled share for a decade; its execution problems (2023 to 2026) paused this.
  • Towards the advantaged: product-led gains (Esteem Body; ConvaFoam winning over 50 percent of US evaluations; GentleCath Air for Women more than doubling) show the better product wins nurses and evaluations.
  • US distribution: competitive bidding pushes Medicare volume from non-contracted distributors (about a third of the market) to a handful of national contractors; vertically integrated manufacturer-distributors with low patient-acquisition cost (Convatec through 180 Medical; Coloplast through Comfort Medical) are judged best placed (DNB Carnegie, Jefferies). Integrated players can also switch patients to own-brand catheters (own-brand share of wallet at 180 Medical rose from 54 to 59 percent since 2022 on one measure, 45 to 55 percent on another).
  • Wound: hospital procurement is consolidating vendors and favours broad portfolios (Solventum, Smith & Nephew, Mölnlycke); Convatec is mid-portfolio.
  • Infusion: each new pump or drug-device that specifies Convatec's set in its regulatory filing adds to its lead.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C+6810th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return first, or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; do prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: competitors put margin first and behave rationally; prices do not fall when volume falls, they drift down about 1 percent a year regardless, and competition is fought with clinical evidence, rebates, portfolio tying and free product for hospital placements rather than list price. This supports stable margins; the price pressure comes from payers and distributors, not from rivals.

  • Coloplast: family control (67 to 68 percent of votes) underwrites patience; it targets a mid-20s margin and protects it; it gives products away virtually free to win hospital placements because the placement, not the unit, is the asset.
  • Hollister: private and employee-owned; dominant in US ostomy; stable.
  • Smith & Nephew and Mölnlycke: both restructuring or reallocating for margin and cash (Mölnlycke paid EUR 200m to its owner Investor AB).
  • Exceptions to rational behaviour: Medline's "good enough" private label in wholesale and post-acute wound; Indian skin-substitute makers at as low as $20 per square centimetre; Chinese locals online.
  • Industry pricing: like-for-like flat to slightly negative; premium mix worth 1 to 2 percent a year; European national tenders and US group-purchasing rebates set the floor.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C+6010th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: is there "Michael O'Leary" instability, and would competitors launch a "shark attack" on pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the attitude is stable and predictable, with no O'Leary-type behaviour and no price shark attack in evidence; the change over three years is a modest shift towards spending for share in the US, led by Coloplast's new chief executive, which will show up as higher commercial intensity and rebates rather than lower list prices.

  • Last 18 months to three years: more price pressure at the contract level (group-purchasing concessions cost Convatec 27 basis points of gross margin in 2025; competitive bidding forces another round in 2028); the premium end remains evidence-led.
  • Coloplast under Gavin Wood (from May 2026): a commercial builder from J&J and Mölnlycke; "Funding the Journey" reinvests about 80 percent of DKK 500m into innovation and commercial expansion; the margin target is now flat at mid-20s, so management has chosen not to maximise near-term margin in order to fund growth. This is the most likely source of rising intensity against Convatec in US ostomy acute accounts in 2027.
  • Would a rival attack if Convatec stumbled? Not on price, because price does not win a nurse's choice and reimbursement caps the gain. The realistic "attack" is aggressive GPO bidding, portfolio rebates and free acute product to take discharges, which erodes Convatec's US ostomy recovery over years.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C6412th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the industry structure is supportive for about 70 percent of revenue (concentrated, rational, sticky) and only moderately supportive for the 30 percent in wound care; the support is robust against competitors and fragile against one actor, the payer, which can reset a category's price by rule. For the model, structure justifies stable gross margins and low churn, and the payer justifies explicit price-event scenarios.

  • Robust: concentrated triopolies in ostomy and continence; a near-monopoly in infusion; high entry barriers (clinical evidence, MDR certification, nurse training networks of 230,000 professionals a year, regulatory filings that name the set); share stability; rational rivals.
  • Fragile: US Medicare (skin substitutes done; competitive bidding 2028; 7 percent of group sales direct); European payers (2027 reforms); distributors that also compete (Medline); procurement-led wound tenders.
  • Shift in the balance of value-chain power: towards integrated manufacturer-distributors with direct patient relationships and away from unintegrated distributors (Business Foundations research), which favours Convatec's 180 Medical model even as its price falls.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?B+625th of 32full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C6211th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a low threat over four years and a slowly rising one over ten; the nearest-term substitute pressure is in infusion (tubeless pumps) and female continence (external catheters), neither of which is yet visible in Convatec's growth.

  • Ostomy: better surgery, colorectal screening and inflammatory bowel disease drugs slowly reduce new stomas; decades-long.
  • Continence: external female catheters (BD's PureWick) for some users; neuromodulation implants for overactive bladder (Coloplast's InTibia, delayed to 2027/28) address a different population from neurogenic intermittent catheter users; ultra-compact catheters are product-level substitution within the category.
  • Infusion: tubeless patch pumps (Convatec now supplies one); oral or inhaled therapies for some drugs in the long run; in Parkinson's, subcutaneous pumps are themselves the substitute gaining share from oral therapy and deep-brain stimulation.
  • Wound: single-use negative pressure therapy and biologics substitute for dressings in hard-to-heal wounds; biologics are now capped in price; Convatec is entering both negative pressure (ConvaVac) and a new category (ConvaNiox).
  • Expert view: surgical or genetic therapies could eventually reduce the need for continence and ostomy consumables (long-term).

Memo topic 3, : the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5623rd of 328 answers
Judgement56C-4162C-4252C-4355C-4460C-4532C-4642C-4760
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5623rd of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Convatec's competitive advantage is real but narrow: two hard moats (infusion sets, 180 Medical) in its fastest-growing franchises, and industry-shared soft advantages in the larger ostomy, continence and wound categories where it is a capable challenger, not the leader. Inferiority is disproved by five years of above-market growth and by named product wins; pricing power is absent except through premium mix, because payers set prices. Advantage is widening slowly through products, contracts and automation, which supports share gains of one to three points a year in growth terms and mechanically improving costs, not a step change. For the model: allow above-market volume growth backed by named products and contracts; assume no like-for-like price above zero; and do not let the hard moats in Infusion and 180 Medical persuade the model that the group has pricing power, because the larger half of revenue does not.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D6220th of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Convatec holds two hard moats, sole-source infusion sets and the 180 Medical captive US channel, and a set of softer, industry-shared advantages (nurse-gatekeeper switching costs, regulatory barriers, adhesive know-how) in ostomy, continence and wound. The hard moats are widening; the soft advantages are real but shared with stronger incumbents, so they protect the base rather than take share. The one moat that narrowed (premium-priced wound biologics) is gone, and the captive channel's price, not its share, is the next to be tested.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

F5224th of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Convatec is a mid-sized player that has scale where it leads and lacks it where it trails: decisive scale in infusion sets and US home delivery, and sub-scale against Coloplast in ostomy and continence, which is the main reason its gross margin is about seven points lower.

  • Absolute size: revenue $2,439m in FY2025, 10,910 employees, over 1 billion consumable units a year in about 90 countries. Coloplast had DKK 28,941m of revenue in FY2025/26 (roughly 1.8 times Convatec); Smith & Nephew $6,164m (its wound unit, $1,793m, is more than twice Convatec's Advanced Wound Care at $753m); Solventum and Mölnlycke are larger in wound.
  • What scale confers where Convatec has it: in infusion, unit cost, manufacturing reliability and the trust needed to be written into partners' drug-device filings; in US continence, billing infrastructure, patient-acquisition cost and the ability to win national bidding contracts.
  • What its lack costs it: adjusted gross margin of 59.4 to 60.7 percent against Coloplast's 65.5 to 67 percent; less commercial density per hospital territory; historically less nurse engagement; a smaller installed base of ostomy patients.
  • R&D in absolute terms: about $100m to $120m a year (about 5 percent of sales), similar in dollars to Coloplast's (3 to 4 percent of a larger base), so scale does not cap innovation spending.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F6025th of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: inferiority is disproved across the portfolio and leadership is demonstrated in specific products: infusion sets, Hydrofiber antimicrobial dressings, soft convex ostomy (Esteem Body), foam (ConvaFoam) and the ConvaNiox trial data. In the portfolio as a whole Convatec is a capable follower, not the clinical leader, per expert calls.

  • Evidence of leading utility: ConvaNiox healed 60 percent more diabetic foot ulcers with three times faster area reduction in a randomised trial (over 600 patients treated across six European countries); ConvaFoam wins over half of US evaluations and lifted global foam share from 5 to about 6 percent; Esteem Body reached about 15 percent of its US segment in under two years; GentleCath Air for Women more than doubled and is over 10 percent of the US compact segment; 180 Medical NPS of 80; infusion sets trusted by every major pump maker; Deutsche Bank reads European wound growth as reassuringly ahead of competitors.
  • Evidence of follower status: expert calls describe Convatec as a market follower lacking disruptive technology, with slower innovation cycles than Coloplast and Hollister; historical loss of stoma share on weak nurse engagement; continence as its weakest manufacturing portfolio; 80 percent of advanced wound products largely interchangeable; slow adoption of digital tools.
  • Disproof of inferiority, the test that matters: five consecutive years of organic growth above the market (6.4 percent excluding InnovaMatrix in FY2025 against a market of 4 to 5 percent) and the organic gap to Coloplast narrowed by about 3 points.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3230th of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: Convatec does not have demonstrable pricing power in the sense the question means; it has moved from about 1 percent annual price erosion to a small positive (+30 basis points) through a pricing centre of excellence, below inflation, and its only real price lever is mix through premium launches. In reimbursed chronic care the payer, not the maker, sets the price.

  • Evidence applied, not conjectured: +30 basis points of price in FY2025 and H1 2026; GPO concessions cost 27 basis points of gross margin in 2025; no indexation in diabetes contracts; three-year GPO terms.
  • Where price is earned: at launch, through evidence (ConvaNiox at £40 per dressing on the UK Drug Tariff; soft convex and hydrophilic premiums). This is mix, worth perhaps 1 to 2 points a year if launches succeed, the same mechanism Coloplast uses (like-for-like flat to negative, premium mix +1 to 2 percent).
  • Where price is taken away: CMS skin substitutes (-85 percent); competitive bidding (-30 percent retail, about -15 percent wholesale, 2028); France and Germany (2027).
  • Sector rule: where a public payer sets the price, the maker waits on the payer; where the patient pays, a better product earns a higher price. Convatec is almost entirely the former.
