P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C587th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below operating profit the story reverses from the last six years. From FY2019 to FY2024 the share count crept up about 0.6 percent a year and interest swung with refinancing; from FY2026 the share count falls about 3.8 percent a year, interest rises steadily with net debt, the tax rate is stable at about 24 percent, and the legacy amortisation has ended. The result: adjusted net income grows about 8.8 percent a year (FY2025 to FY2030), about the same as adjusted operating profit (8.6 percent), while adjusted EPS grows about 13 percent, the 4-point gap being almost all buybacks. Reported EPS roughly doubles from 8.6 cents (FY2025) to about 21 cents (FY2027) with no change in the business, because the $93m to $95m a year of spin-out amortisation ended in Jul 2026. Free cash flow grows faster than earnings, from about $219m (FY2025, legacy definition) to about $451m (FY2030), as capital expenditure falls from 8.4 to about 5.6 percent of sales, but FY2026 is weak (about $233m) and the cash tax rate is the main unmodelled risk.

  • EPS against the last four to six years. Adjusted EPS grew about 11.5 percent a year from FY2022 (12.7 cents) to FY2025 (17.6 cents), almost all from margin, with no help from the share count until the 2025 buyback. The base case grows it about 13 percent a year to FY2030, of which revenue about 6.1 points, margin about 2.3 points, below-EBIT items about zero (rising interest offset by a stable tax rate) and the share count about 3.9 points. The composition moves from self-help margin, which the market discounts as execution risk, towards revenue and mechanical capital return.
  • Net income grows more slowly than consensus in FY2027 to FY2028 (429 and 461 against 444 and 498) because the base case's revenue, margin and interest are all slightly less favourable; EPS is closer to consensus (22.7 against 23.0; 25.2 against 26.0) because the buyback offsets about half the gap.
  • Free cash flow. FY2026 is held down by peak capital expenditure (about $215m; growth $135m to $165m), the H1 working-capital build ($146m outflow; net working capital 19.0 percent of trailing revenue at H1 against 17.6 percent a year earlier) and the cost of the $200m buyback's debt. From FY2028 capital expenditure falls to about $170m to $185m (5.6 to 6.0 percent of sales; guided 5 to 7 percent from 2028), which with rising EBITDA lifts free cash flow about 25 percent in FY2028. The FactSet consensus free cash flow ($230m, $338m, $403m) is on a narrower definition (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure only), so the base case's FY2027 to FY2028 figures are about $40m to $45m lower on like-for-like items, mainly intangible capital expenditure, leases and the cash tax assumption.
  • Reported earnings convergence matters for perception, not for value. Reported EPS should rise to about 21 cents in FY2027 from 8.6 cents in FY2025. FactSet-based screens and "reported P/E" will show the stock suddenly cheaper; this helps the "earnings quality" narrative the first set identified (C-54), and it arrives mechanically in the FY2027 prints (Aug 2027 interim; Feb 2028 full year), with a first glimpse in the H2 2026 numbers on 23 Feb 2027.

Risks to these lines. A bolt-on acquisition above about $300m would replace two years of buyback accretion (about 2 to 3 percent of EPS) with deal risk on a weak record; sterling strength reduces pence EPS (about -1 percent for each 1 percent rise in GBP/USD, on translation alone) and the number of shares each dollar buys; the cash tax rate could converge faster or slower than assumed (±$20m to $25m a year of free cash flow); a CMS audit or retroactive-repayment action on distributors (DNB Carnegie flags this as overlooked) would hit 180 Medical's cash flow, not adjusted EPS.

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

C+649th of 32

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is plausible scope for about 200 to 300 basis points of margin over 2025 to 2028 and for buyback-driven EPS growth beyond consensus, and peer precedents show margins in the mid-20s to low-30s are achievable in this industry. The improvement comes from a few big areas (mix towards Infusion and premium launches, the end of the InnovaMatrix drag, operating leverage on 6 to 8 percent growth) plus many small ones, and it is fully congruent with Accelerate.

  • Big areas: mix (Infusion is margin-accretive; premium soft convex, hydrophilic and ConvaNiox products); InnovaMatrix's drag (140 basis points in H1 2026) gone from 2027; operating leverage on fixed overheads; buybacks (C-19).
  • Many small: delayering, procurement, pricing centre gains, Esenta accessories (about 20 percent of ostomy revenue), digital commercial tools.
  • Peer precedents: Coloplast at 27 to 33 percent EBIT through the 2010s (now mid-20s); Smith & Nephew's wound margin +240 basis points to 24.9 percent; Mölnlycke about 24 percent; Bernstein forecasts Convatec's adjusted EBITA margin up about 320 basis points through 2030 against about 140 for Coloplast.
  • Congruence: the execution-excellence pillar; double-digit EPS ambition.

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D4824th of 32

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months?

Judgement: the reported growth rate has decelerated sharply and the underlying rate has decelerated mildly, but the input engine itself (the product cycle) has accelerated. The deceleration is explained by InnovaMatrix and by phasing in Infusion and wound orders, and it is due to reverse in H2 2026; if the ten-month update does not show the reversal, the explanation weakens and the risk rises sharply.

  • Output: group organic growth 7.7 percent (FY2024), 4.8 percent (FY2025), 1.6 percent (four months to Apr 2026), 1.8 percent (H1 2026). Excluding InnovaMatrix: about 6.7 percent (prior four-month period), 6.4 percent (FY2025), 5.0 percent (H1 2026), below the company's normal 6 to 7 percent.
  • By segment, H1 2026 against FY2025: Infusion 7.4 percent against 12.5 percent (customer order phasing); Continence 5.9 against 6.6 percent; Ostomy 4.3 against 4.5 percent; Advanced Wound Care excluding InnovaMatrix 3.4 against 4.1 percent (a softer wound market and Aquacel order phasing in May and Jun).
  • Input: Wave 1 products supplied over half of H1 organic growth; Esteem Body $60m annualised; GentleCath Air for Women doubled; ConvaFoam share up; six Wave 2 products launching in 2026.
  • Test: H2 2026 organic growth of 6 to 8 percent excluding InnovaMatrix, Infusion at double digits.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C+556th of 23

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the net effect is a business that grows revenue faster than in the last six years (about 6.1 percent a year to FY2030 against 4.9 percent from FY2019 to FY2025), expands margin about half as fast (about 55 basis points a year against about 115), grows adjusted EPS at about the same pace (about 13 percent a year against about 11.5 to 14.6 percent), and roughly doubles free cash flow. The quality of the EPS growth changes: less from cost self-help, more from revenue (Infusion and launches) and from buybacks under a fixed leverage policy. Two years stand out as breaks in the trend: FY2026 (reported growth +4.6 percent, the low point, with a back-end-loaded margin) and FY2028 (competitive bidding takes about 1 point of revenue and about 50 basis points of margin). The forecast is therefore not a straight line, and the 18-month window contains the hardest single step (H2 2026) and the cleanest optics (H1 2027 is the first half without the InnovaMatrix drag).

The net effect, line by line, of the six forces assessed in this set.

  • Operating leverage (P-01): +55 basis points a year of margin on average, back-end loaded within FY2026 and front-loaded across the period (FY2027 the best year).
  • Financial leverage (P-02): about +1.5 percent a year of EPS net of interest; balance sheet held at about 2.0 times.
  • Cost elimination (P-03): about $30m to $35m a year gross, of which about $11m to $17m a year net reaches the margin to FY2028; most of it pays for inflation, depreciation and reinvestment rather than adding margin on its own.
  • Price (P-04): about -0.3 points a year of revenue on average and about -60 to -90 basis points of margin cumulatively, mostly in FY2028.
  • Feedback loops (P-05): about +1.5 to +2.5 points a year of revenue growth above the market from Infusion and launches; balancing loops on price and selling cost.
  • Below the line (P-08): stable tax, rising interest, falling share count; reported earnings converge on adjusted from FY2027.

Where the cone is widest. In FY2026 the cone is narrow on revenue and narrow-but-skewed-down on margin; in FY2027 it widens on gross margin (inflation) and on Wave 2 launch traction; in FY2028 it is widest, on one dated decision (bidding, about ±3.5 to 4.5 percent of EPS) plus the margin path. The third set should test concentration: about 60 percent of the forecast difference against the last four years (the faster revenue and the share-count fall) rests on two drivers, Infusion volume and the buyback policy; the margin climb rests mainly on one, the H2 2026 to 2027 overhead and automation bridge.

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

–54

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the gap between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect is small, and today it became smaller. Guidance was raised on growth, the shares rose 9 percent, and my base case moved up to consensus rather than consensus moving up to anything new. There is no earnings gap for delivery to close in FY2026 or FY2027: guidance sits on the floor the company will most likely hit, consensus sits on guidance, and my forecasts sit on consensus. What remains is a gap in timing and in composition, and it is favourable for about nine months and less favourable after that.

Favourable, and mostly unreflected, to about August 2027. A first half of 2027 that will look much stronger than the second (revenue up about 7.4 percent, margin up about 180 basis points, EPS up about 20 percent or more), because it laps a depressed first half of 2026. A buyback of $200m to $250m most likely announced then, which only one broker models at all. The FY2026 margin print on 23 Feb 2027, which I put at about four in five to meet the floor. A bidding deferral at about three in ten. Reported EPS roughly doubling with no change in the business.

Unfavourable, and mostly unreflected, from late summer 2027. The CMS payment amounts, which most likely confirm that bidding proceeds while FY2028 estimates carry only part of it. A second half of 2027 that laps about 9 percent growth and about 20 percent in Infusion. Buyback accretion that is about 1.5 percent a year net of interest against a narrative that quotes the gross figure. Wound-care estimates that are too high. European price measures and a French levy the company has not addressed. A growth range for 2027 whose midpoint sits above recent delivery for the first time.

What this means for the memo.

  • Guidance and estimates (topics 4 and 7): describe a company that raises growth guidance late in the year and meets margin guidance at the floor, with scope for small upgrades (central case about plus 1 percent to FY2027 consensus) rather than an upgrade cycle. The conservatism in growth guidance is thinner than its history implies.
  • Momentum (topic 8): operating momentum is at its best in a year, but it will not show in dollar revision counts, and the share price has taken it in one day.
  • Stage (topic 10): the stock has moved from "turning up" to the start of "thesis broadening": worries have cleared one by one since September (the bidding bill, the new chief financial officer, Coloplast's reset, and now the second-half growth) and the first near-term guidance raise under the new chief executive has happened. The broadening most likely peaks around the August 2027 results; the six months after that carry the risk of looking like a stall for reasons of comparison and of Medicare timing, not of the business.
  • Multiple and return (topics 11 and 12): the earnings part of the return is about 15 percent over the 13 months to the end of 2027 and is reliable. The rest is the multiple, which has already risen from about 11.2 to about 14.2 times; about half of that came with the sector. A further rise to 14.5 to 16.5 times is defensible on company delivery; the guideline 19.27 times asks for a sector re-rating as well and would value Convatec at about the level of Coloplast. The model's return figures cannot be used until its share count is repaired.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

–58

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: guidance is most likely to be met at each of the next events, with growth guidance the part that gets raised and margin guidance the part that gets met at the floor; today's raise fits that pattern exactly. Over the 18 months I put about 45 percent on at least one further raise of in-year growth guidance, about 30 percent on at least one reduction of a headline number, and about 35 percent on neither. Three things shape the sequence. The cushion in growth guidance is thinner than the record suggests. 2027 is a year of two unequal halves, so the natural moment for a raise is August 2027. And the next buyback is more likely to be announced in August 2027 than on 23 Feb 2027. For 18 months to three years out, the growth target is about as likely to be qualified for competitive bidding as kept, and the margin target is more likely to be held at "mid-20s" than raised.

The record, read for the pattern (organic growth; 2025 and 2026 exclude InnovaMatrix):

Year Initial range Changed at Delivered Delivered less initial midpoint
2020 2.0 to 3.5% none recorded 4.0% +1.25 points
2021 3.0 to 4.5% Raised in July to 3.5 to 5.0% 5.3% +1.55
2022 4.0 to 5.5% none recorded 5.6% +0.85
2023 4.5 to 6.0% Raised in August to 5.0 to 7.0% 7.2% +1.95
2024 5.0 to 7.0% Raised in November to 7.25 to 8.0% 7.7% +1.7
2025 5.0 to 7.0% Narrowed in November to 6.0 to 6.5% 6.4% +0.4
2026 5.0 to 7.0% Narrowed in August to 5.5 to 6.5%; raised today to 6.25 to 7.0% about 6.7% (my estimate) about +0.7
  • The cushion has shrunk as the bar has risen. While the initial midpoint was 2.75 to 6.0 percent, delivery beat it by 0.85 to 1.95 points. In the two years since the midpoint reached 6.0 percent, the beat has been 0.4 and about 0.7 points. If FY2027 is guided at the mid-term range of 6 to 8 percent, its midpoint (7.0 percent) will sit above what the business delivered in 2025 and 2026 for the first time. The "sandbagging precision" the first set describes is a fair reading of 2020 to 2024, not of what comes next. Consensus at about 7 percent leaves no cushion; my 6.5 percent is in the lower half of the range.
  • When raises come. In-year changes have come at the half-year (2021, 2023) or at the ten-month update (2024, 2025, 2026), never at the full-year results or the four-month update.
  • Margin. The floor has stepped up by about 100 basis points a year (at least 19.7 percent for FY2023, raised in-year to at least 20.5 percent in constant currency; at least 21.0 percent for FY2024; at least 23.0 percent for FY2026) and results have landed on it or just above it (21.2 percent in FY2024, 22.3 percent in FY2025). "At least 24.0 percent" is the natural FY2027 floor and coincides with the bottom of the target. The floor was raised in-year once in seven years.
  • A note on the first set's record. It gives FY2022 guidance as 4.5 to 6.0 percent growth and a margin floor above 19.7 percent. On the AlphaSense evidence both are the opening FY2023 guidance, placed a year early. Corrected, FY2022 was a beat on growth and a met floor on margin.

The events, with my probabilities:

Event Raised or beaten Met or restated Reduced or missed What drives it
23 Feb 2027: FY2026 outcome against guidance Growth above 7.0%: 10%; margin 23.2% or more: 20% Growth in range: 85%; margin 23.0 to 23.1%: 58% Margin below 23.0%: 22%; growth below range: 5% November and December need only 4.6 percent growth for the bottom of the range; the margin needs flat second-half operating costs and currency no worse
23 Feb 2027: FY2027 guidance against the mid-term targets Above (for example a floor above 24.0%): 10% At the targets (6 to 8%; at least 24.0% or "mid-20s"; double-digit EPS): 70% Below (a floor under 24.0%, or a lower growth range): 20% The inflation assumption once 2027 hedges are known; the French levy; how much launch spending management chooses
May 2027: four-month update 5% 90% 5% An easy comparative; no history of changes at this event
Early Aug 2027: H1 2027 results Growth narrowed to the upper half or raised: 30% 55% 15% H1 organic growth of about 7.5 percent and a margin up about 180 basis points on H1 2026; against that, management will know the second-half comparatives
Nov 2027: ten-month update 25% 60% 15% Infusion against a second half of 2026 that grew about 20 percent; Natura Body's first months; validation of the new line
Feb 2028: FY2027 outcome, FY2028 guidance, new mid-term framework 15% 55% 30% Competitive bidding in the FY2028 numbers; whether the margin reached 24.0 percent

2027 is a year of two unequal halves. H1 2026 was depressed on every line: growth of 5.0 percent excluding InnovaMatrix, Infusion at 7.4 percent, a margin of 21.2 percent carrying 140 basis points of InnovaMatrix drag. July to October 2026 was the opposite: about 9 percent, with Infusion at about 20 percent. So in my base case H1 2027 shows revenue up about 7.4 percent, an adjusted operating margin of about 23.0 percent (up about 180 basis points) and EPS up about 20 percent or more, while H2 2027 shows revenue up about 5.5 percent, a margin of about 25.0 percent (up about 40 basis points) and EPS up about 7 to 8 percent. Nothing in the business changes between the halves; only the comparatives do. Two cautions: the split assumes flat currency (at today's stronger dollar, H1 2027's reported growth would be lower than its organic growth, because H1 2026 carried about 2.6 points of currency benefit), and Infusion's 2027 growth depends on when the new capacity is validated. The consequence for guidance is that the August 2027 results are the point of greatest strength, and the November 2027 update and the February 2028 results are where growth will look as if it is slowing.

The next buyback: August, not February. Both programmes so far were launched in August (2025, completed by December; 2026, announced with the interim results on 4 Aug). The reason is seasonal: the first half absorbs cash (a working-capital outflow of $146m and free cash flow to equity of only $22m in H1 2026) and the second half releases it. On 23 Feb 2027 the company will be at about 2.1 times leverage, with the cash-hungry half ahead and a chief financial officer who starts six days later. I put about one in four on a new buyback being announced in February and about three in four on one by August, most likely of $200m to $250m. Both earlier sets treat 23 Feb 2027 as the moment a buyback "is or is not announced"; I disagree, and the memo should not set up its absence in February as a disappointment.

Eighteen months to three years out.

  • Growth target (6 to 8 percent). If bidding is deferred (about three in ten) the target is simply kept. If it proceeds, the company must fit a 1 to 2 percent headwind into FY2028: I put about three in ten on it holding 6 to 8 percent unqualified and about seven in ten on a qualification ("excluding competitive bidding") or a lower FY2028 range. Overall: kept about 50 percent, qualified or reduced about 49 percent, raised almost nil.
  • Margin target. Held at "mid-20s" about 65 percent; raised to a higher ambition for 2030 about 20 percent; trimmed or delayed about 15 percent. Coloplast has just chosen a flat mid-20s margin to fund growth, Convatec's chief executive said today that "we are investing to accelerate growth", and half of the long-term incentive rests on EPS growth, which buybacks and revenue can deliver without a higher margin.
  • Double-digit EPS growth. Kept about 80 percent; it needs buybacks from FY2028, which the policy supplies.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

–52

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: consensus EPS is most likely to be held, with small upgrades to FY2027 through August 2027 and a risk of small cuts to FY2028 from late summer 2027; the net breadth of revisions should be positive but shallow for the next nine months and mixed after that. My base case is level with consensus for FY2026 and FY2027 and about 2 percent below it for FY2028, so delivery alone closes no gap. The totals agree while the composition does not: consensus has higher operating profit, lower interest and more shares than I do, and a wound-care business that is too high offset by an Infusion business that is too low.

What today's update does to estimates.

  • FY2026: almost nothing at the EPS line. Consensus already has revenue of $2,553m, a margin of 23.0 percent ($588m), finance costs of about $75m (pre-tax profit $513m) and EPS of 20.0 cents; today's guidance maps onto the same figures, because the organic upgrade is offset by currency. Brokers below the floor (DNB Carnegie at 22.8 percent) come up a little. I expect changes of 0 to plus 1 percent.
  • The upgrade is invisible in the revision data. FactSet's revisions are in reported dollars. Organic growth goes up about 0.6 points and the currency benefit comes down about 0.4 to 0.6 points, so "sales revisions" stay near zero. The dashboard's sell-side momentum rating (C; FY2027 EPS up 1, 1, 2 and 3 percent over one, three, six and twelve months; sales 0, 0, minus 1 and plus 1) may barely move even though the operating news is the best in a year. The Consumer should read the organic figures, not the revision counts.
  • The mix inside the estimates should change. FactSet's segment consensus for FY2026 has Infusion at $520m (I have about $537m), Advanced Wound Care at $739m (about $724m), Ostomy at $722m (about $715m, on currency) and Continence at $574m (about $576m). Wound care is the consensus line most exposed to cuts, in FY2027 as well ($788m against my $757m).
  • Target prices and ratings. The median target of $4.02 is about 305p at today's exchange rate, 14 percent above 268p; targets will be raised to keep the recommendations. With every FactSet broker already at Buy or Overweight, ratings can only move one way, and the first "hold on valuation" note becomes possible as the price approaches the targets.

The likely path of consensus EPS, event by event:

Event FY2027 (23.0 cents) FY2028 (26.0 cents) Breadth
Now to year-end 0 to +1% 0 to +1% Positive, shallow
23 Feb 2027 Held if the margin is 23.0 to 23.1 percent and the FY2027 floor is 24.0 percent; +1 to +2% on a margin of 23.2 percent or more; -2 to -4% from every broker at once on a miss (about one in five) Same direction Flat, with a tail
Aug 2027 +1 to +2% (H1 strength; buyback added net of interest) +1 to +2% The most positive point

Over the 18 months as a whole I expect FY2027 consensus EPS to finish between minus 2 and plus 3.5 percent of today's 23.0 cents (central case plus 1 percent) and FY2028 between minus 4 and plus 3 percent of 26.0 cents (central case minus 1 percent). In pence, a weaker pound adds to this (about 1.5 percent since the earlier sets were written).

Why the totals agree and the parts do not (FY2028). Consensus has EBIT of $717m against my $701m; pre-tax profit of $658m against $615m, because its interest falls to about $59m while mine rises to $86m; net income of $498m against $468m (6 percent lower); and about 1,915m shares against my 1,830m, which brings EPS back to within 2 percent. Three consequences follow.

  • Buyback upgrades will be smaller than the narrative suggests. When a broker adds a buyback it must also add the interest. Deutsche Bank already does this: it left its EPS unchanged on 5 Aug 2026 because higher interest offset the accretion. Morgan Stanley's "10 percent EPS tailwind over three years" is gross. The net figure is about 1.5 percent a year. Only DNB Carnegie publishes explicit successor buybacks ($100m in FY2027, $150m in FY2028), well below what the policy implies ($225m and $275m in my inputs).
  • Consensus operating profit for FY2028 is the vulnerable line. It carries a gross margin of about 61.3 percent (five brokers), above every recent year (61.0 percent in FY2024, 60.7 percent in FY2025), and only part of competitive bidding.
  • The second set's reading stands: consensus is internally consistent with deleveraging, not with the stated policy.

Eighteen months to three years out. FY2029 consensus (29.1 cents, four brokers) is level with my 29.0 cents. The forces are the same ones, larger: buybacks of $275m to $350m a year net of interest (plus 1.5 percent a year), against competitive bidding, a consensus gross margin that looks too high, and the long-run question of tubeless pumps in Infusion (Bernstein has cut its long-term Infusion growth to 5 percent; DNB Carnegie has 10 percent). Most likely: FY2028 held to slightly cut, FY2029 held.

D-0758
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

–58

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: day to day the share price is mostly its own (the peer basket explains 21 percent of daily variance over the latest six months and 15 percent on average over two years), but over the multi-month horizon that sets the multiple about half of the move has been the sector's. The level of the multiple, a ten-year trough, is a sector fact; the path since September and the gap to Coloplast are company facts. The multiple is therefore roughly half company-driven. Convatec can earn its way from about 14 to about 16 times on its own delivery; the guideline 19.27 times would also need the sector's valuation wave.

The evidence.

  • Daily co-movement is low but has risen. Over the six months to 17 Nov 2026 the correlation with the equal-weighted peer basket is 0.46, the beta 0.70, and the basket explains 21 percent of daily variance; 79 percent is the stock's own. Across 24 month-end readings the explained share ranges from 1 to 32 percent and averages 15 percent, but it was about 1 percent until March 2025 and has averaged 21 percent over the last twelve readings. Since US payer proposals and tariffs made reimbursed medtech a shared trade in spring 2025, Convatec has had a sector component it did not have before. The closest pairs are Smith & Nephew (0.48), Medacta (0.40), Alcon (0.39) and Ypsomed (0.35). Coloplast, the most relevant peer, has no price history in the table.
  • Over six months about half the move was the sector's. The stock rose 21.2 percent and the basket 15.2 percent. At a beta of 0.70 the basket accounts for about 10.6 points and the company for about 10.6. Consensus FY2027 EPS rose only 2 percent over the same period, so nearly all of the rise was the multiple: about half sector, about half Convatec's own cleared worries (the bidding bill on 9 Oct, the new chief financial officer on 3 Nov, Coloplast's reset on the same day). Today's 9 percent rise, on a day the index fell, is wholly its own.
  • The level is the sector's. Sixteen of the 22 rated stocks in the dashboard sector carry a valuation rating of A, and the ten-year P/E percentiles of the closest peers are all at the bottom of their ranges (Convatec 2, Coloplast 3, Smith & Nephew 3, Alcon 1, Straumann 2). Being cheap against its own history does not distinguish Convatec.
  • The relative re-rating is already well advanced. Coloplast's premium to Convatec was about 70 percent historically and 55 percent on a five-year average (Jefferies). Recent broker notes put it at 20 to 40 percent (Morgan Stanley 20 percent in September; JP Morgan 33 percent on 21 Oct, at 18.3 against 13.8 times 2027 earnings; Jefferies 40 percent in November), and Convatec has risen about 12 percent since JP Morgan's figure. The company-driven part of a re-rating means closing a gap to Coloplast that has already more than halved; beyond that, Convatec would have to trade through the market leader.

What this means for the multiple the memo uses. At 19.27 times Convatec would be valued at about the 18 to 20 times at which brokers put Coloplast today and above its own five-year 90th percentile (18.88 times), and it would have to get there in 13 months from about 14.2 times. Company delivery alone does not support that: it supports a multiple between the two-year median (14.3 times) and the five-year median (16.55 times), and I regard 14.5 to 16.5 times at the end of 2027 as the defensible range, the upper end requiring both a clean FY2026 margin and a sector that keeps recovering. The reverse also holds: with a fifth to a third of its variance now shared, a sector de-rating (a rise in yields, money rotating back out of healthcare, a peer's warning) would take the multiple down whatever Convatec reports. The first set's point that "the leveraged defensive with a contested estimate de-rates first" still applies, although the estimate is less contested than it was yesterday.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

B587th of 32

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in European medtech consumables, long-run share-price outperformance has come from earnings growth that is fast enough to rise through the sector's valuation wave, plus a re-rating of the company's own multiple from a cheap start once worries clear; underperformance has come from payer resets, quality or supply failures, value-destroying acquisitions and slow growth that is "punished twice" (by low growth and by a falling multiple). Convatec fits the outperformance template on the operating side (five years of 5 percent-plus organic growth, 460 basis points of margin, a cheap starting multiple, worries clearing) but its own history is a cautionary instance of the second clock: it was one of the four weakest ten-year share prices in the sector despite growing expected earnings, because its multiple fell faster. What converts the template into share-price outperformance is a beat-and-raise, not a beat.

  • The two clocks (house analysis of 31 Oct 2026): over months a medtech stock follows its own news (only a fifth to a quarter of monthly movement is shared with the group); over years three forces add up: earnings growth (the most reliable route over ten years), the stock's own re-rating or de-rating (almost as important), and a valuation wave moving the group. Rank correlation of share price with expected earnings change was 0.57 to 0.81 by period. The four weakest ten-year share prices (Fresenius, Smith & Nephew, Elekta, Convatec) all grew expected earnings 12 to 51 percent, too slowly to offset a falling multiple. Winners such as Getinge (multiple about 11 to 16 times) and Oxford Instruments (13 to 23 times) re-rated from cheap starts.
  • Rules of thumb from the sector notes: a results beat alone does not lift a share price; the first beat-and-raise does. At the bottom of a range, a franchise's multiple can rise without an earnings upgrade as each worry clears, as long as the business underneath is intact (Morgan Stanley: European medtech typically rebounds through multiple expansion after a major sell-off). Where a public payer sets the price, the maker waits on the payer.
  • Precedents of outperformance in chronic care: Coloplast in the 2010s compounded 7 to 8 percent organic growth at 30 percent-plus margins with ROIC near 45 percent and earned a 30-plus times multiple.
  • Precedents of underperformance: Coloplast since 2021 (acquisitions of Atos and Kerecis took ROIC from about 45 to 15 percent, margin from 33 to 25.7 percent, the forward P/E from 32 to about 15 to 20 times, consensus EPS down about 30 percent in three years); Smith & Nephew (restructuring self-help without share recovery, a persistent discount); Convatec itself after its 2016 IPO (two profit warnings in 2017 on supply and packaging failures, the shares 40 percent below the 225p IPO price, chief executive and chair out); payer events (the 2025 Medicare proposal drove a 25 percent fall in Convatec before CMS withdrew it in Dec 2025; skin-substitute caps hit Convatec, Coloplast and Smith & Nephew together); Philips Respironics (a quality failure that ceded 90 percent of US CPAP to ResMed).
  • Where Convatec fits: the operating template is there (growth, margin, cash returns, an identifiable cleared worry); the multiple is cheap against its own history (24-month-forward 13.4 times, the 12th percentile of five years, median 16.55 times) though no longer extreme after the 16 percent move since Sep 2026; the worries are clearing (InnovaMatrix written off, CFO appointed, bidding bill introduced). It does not yet fit the "beat-and-raise" trigger: guidance has been narrowed, not raised, and consensus sits on guidance. It also carries the "slow growth punished twice" risk: at 6 to 8 percent organic and about 13 percent EPS growth it needs its own re-rating, not just its earnings, to outperform.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

B425th of 32

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: to a fair trough multiple of about 10.5 to 11.0 times 24-month-forward earnings on unchanged consensus, the shares could fall about 18 to 22 percent, to roughly 198p to 207p; with a credible earnings miss as well (an H2 2026 margin shortfall plus 2027 inflation, cutting forward EPS by 5 to 8 percent), about 26 to 28 percent, to roughly 182p to 188p; the brokers' published bear cases of 170p to 180p imply 29 to 33 percent. The downside has roughly doubled since the memo because the price has risen 16 percent while earnings have barely moved.

  • Today: 252.8p (16 Nov 2026); FactSet 24-month-forward P/E 13.37 times (13 Nov 2026), the 12th percentile of five years (range 11.16 to 20.54 times, median 16.55 times) and the 30th of the last two years (median 14.31 times); 24-month-forward consensus EPS about 0.188 GBP.
  • Trough anchors: the five-year low of 11.16 times (Sep 2026); the dashboard's ten-year "crash" band of 13.20 times (today's multiple is just above it); Smith & Nephew at 10.4 times; Fresenius at 12.7 times; a sector whose median stock was at the 2nd percentile of its range in Oct 2026.
  • Arithmetic: 11.0 times 0.188 = about 207p (-18 percent); 10.5 times = about 198p (-22 percent); 10.5 times on EPS 5 to 8 percent lower = about 182p to 188p (-26 to -28 percent); the 52-week low is 193p.
  • For balance, the upside anchors: 14.5 times (two-year median area) is about 273p; 16.6 times (ten-year median) about 312p; the median target of 4.02 USD and the dashboard's consensus target of 3.07 GBP imply about 20 percent upside.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

A623rd of 32

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead is a staircase of dated proofs rather than surprises: tomorrow's ten-month update, the FY2026 margin print on 23 Feb 2027, the first InnovaMatrix-free half in Aug 2027 and the 2027 margin target in Feb 2028, with Natura Body and new Infusion capacity in between. These steps are well-founded and will make reported growth look markedly better in 2027, but they are largely in consensus. The novel steps are capital returns beyond 2026 and a competitive-bidding deferral; the novel negative is bidding proceeding in Jan 2028. Since the shares have already risen 16 percent on the clearing of worries (Coloplast's reset, the new chief financial officer, the bidding bill), the staircase now needs a beat-and-raise, not just delivery, to carry the price further.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–68

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the earlier answers are highly congruent on the structure of the case and jar only in a handful of specific, checkable places. Those places cut both ways (the first set was too generous on three mechanical positives; both sets were too cautious on margin delivery), all are now settled or narrowed by evidence, and none changes the direction of the case. The one tension that remains is not between answers but between the case and the price: the earnings forecasts sit on consensus, so the return rests on the multiple.

What every part of the earlier work agrees on, and today's update fits:

  • Demand is structural at 4 to 5 percent and adds nothing to the acceleration; growth above it must be named share gain. The second set built the model exactly that way (Infusion and launches as the load-bearing sources), and today's growth came from exactly there.
  • Price is taken, not made. The lowest scores in the first set (pricing power 32 and 35; regulation 38) and in the second (price 38) say the same thing, and both sets handle the payer as dated scenarios rather than as a trend.
  • The margin gain from here is half the past pace, carried by operating costs rather than gross margin, with inflation, depreciation and reinvestment absorbing much of the self-help.
  • Capital returns under the 2.0 times policy are the mechanical leg of EPS growth.
  • Management is a reliable tracker that executes what it announces.
  • The scores are tightly grouped (group scores of 45 to 62 in the first set; 55 in the second): a "good, not outstanding" business throughout, with no pocket of enthusiasm that other answers contradict.

Where the answers jar, and how each resolves:

Point First set Second set Evidence now Resolution
InnovaMatrix "base effect" in 2027 Adds 2.5 to 3 points to growth and helps consensus Optical: reported FY2027 growth gets almost nothing Company, 18 Nov: InnovaMatrix about $5m in FY2026 Second set is right
Interest-rate sensitivity $10m per 100 basis points $2m (company disclosure) Company annual report Second set is right
End of the 2008 spin-out amortisation Dec 2026 Jul 2026 Company, Aug 2026; two brokers still write Dec 2026 Second set is right

The remaining tension. The dashboard rates valuation A (the P/E is at the 2nd percentile of ten years) and the ratings store rates TSR/Valuation D on the re-based return, and notes that the two disagree. Both are right in their own terms, and the disagreement is the case in miniature: the stock is cheap against its history, the forecasts are consensus, and since 30 Sep the multiple has risen from about 11.2 to about 14.2 times with almost no change in estimates. The first set's C-84 states this openly (it needs its own re-rating, not just its earnings); the Consumer should make sure the memo does too.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

–50

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: the dynamics that decide the next 18 months are comprehensively articulated by the sell-side and, from today, largely priced: the second-half step, the 24 percent margin, the hedge roll-off, competitive bidding, the Infusion story and the closing gap to Coloplast are in every house's notes. What is not articulated is a short list of second-order points, and they do not all point the same way: the net (not gross) effect of buybacks and their August timing, the two unequal halves of 2027, the narrowness of the acceleration, Convatec's specific exposure to the French levy and German pricing, the cash tax rate, and the nature of the FDA letter. I have no direct evidence of investor commentary on social media; what investors think reaches me only through brokers' feedback notes.

The evidence base, and its limit. The AlphaSense narrative report rests on six houses (Bernstein, Deutsche Bank, DNB Carnegie, Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, Rothschild & Co Redburn), and I have JP Morgan through a press report. FactSet names Goldman Sachs, RBC, Barclays, Stifel and Morningstar as well, and one data provider counts 19 analysts; I have seen nothing from those. The map below therefore covers the most detailed houses but well under half of the analysts by number. On the investor side, the only evidence is second-hand: Bernstein reporting that "bearish investors raised the near-term risk to guidance given the implied acceleration" (June), Deutsche Bank's investor breakfast titled "Execution, Execution, Execution" (October), Morgan Stanley's feedback note on its sector initiations (September), and JP Morgan proposing a long Convatec, short Coloplast pair (21 Oct). These say investors' debate was the second-half step; today answered most of it.

What the map says. The well-known dynamics are the large ones, and consensus and the price reflect them. The little-known ones are individually worth 1 to 3 percent of EPS or a few months of timing. Counting them: two lean positive (the strength of the first half of 2027; reported earnings converging), five lean negative (buyback accretion overstated; the second half of 2027; Europe; the FDA tail; cash tax), and one is positive in amount but negative against what is said (buybacks). The narrative is therefore not behind the facts. If anything it is slightly ahead of them on capital returns and on the meaning of today's acceleration.

Eighteen months to three years out. Competitive bidding is fully articulated. ConvaNiox as a possible largest product is management's line and is treated by brokers as an option from 2028. The reset of Coloplast's ambition is known. The less-articulated longer-run points are the shift to tubeless pumps (only Bernstein has cut for it), a second bidding round around 2031, and what margin the industry settles at (the second set's point that 24 to 26 percent is where Coloplast stopped).

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 17 November 2026, 10:52 to 11:12 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Convatec (CTEC-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

  • Which results the latest Earnings Review covers. Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Convatec Group plc (CTEC-GB), a London-listed, US-dollar-reporting maker of chronic-care medical consumables in four segments: Advanced Wound Care, Ostomy Care, Continence Care (including the US home-delivery distributor 180 Medical) and Infusion Care (infusion sets for insulin pumps and subcutaneous Parkinson's therapies).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Coloplast (closest listed chronic-care peer in ostomy, continence and wound; reset its 2030 targets and launched the Funding the Journey programme on 3 Nov 2026); Hollister (private, employee-owned; US ostomy leader); Smith & Nephew (listed wound-care peer running cost, footprint and inventory programmes); Mölnlycke (private, owned by Investor AB; wound-care peer with disclosed margins and cost initiatives); Solventum (listed advanced wound care leader separated from 3M); B. Braun (private ostomy and continence competitor); Ypsomed (listed maker of high-volume drug-delivery consumables with automated manufacturing). Inputs: What share of Convatec's cost base is fixed and what share variable, starting from the cost structure disclosed at the 9 Apr 2026 Capital Markets Day (cost of goods sold about 40 percent of revenue; within it raw materials about 45 percent, overheads 30 percent, labour 25 percent; freight 5 percent and utilities 2 percent of cost of goods sold); how adjusted operating margin and incremental margins moved with organic growth at Convatec in each year from 2019 to H1 2026 (including the 2021 trough of 17.7 percent and the 2022 to 2023 inflation period) and at Coloplast through its 2021 to 2026 margin decline; what the Convatec Business Services programme has delivered against what was promised (G&A from about 13 percent of sales in 2019 to about 6 percent in H1 2026 per the company) and what further savings the fourth centre in Hyderabad and the use of artificial intelligence in finance, quality and supply chain are expected to yield; the status of factory automation (plants reduced from nine in 2022 to five core sites; automated packaging lines needing two operators instead of ten; which of Slovakia, Mexico and the Dominican Republic remain to be automated, on what timetable, and what Deeside's automation has saved); how Convatec's operating costs have moved between H1 and H2 in each year from 2022 to 2025, and what the sell-side and management say is needed in H2 2026 to meet the guided FY2026 adjusted operating margin of at least 23.0 percent after 21.2 percent in H1 2026; which cost lines proved cuttable and which did not in the cost programmes of Coloplast, Smith & Nephew (including its Malaysia plant transfer) and Mölnlycke, and how long they took; whether selling and distribution costs are expected to rise with the 2026 to 2028 launch programme and with Coloplast's stated reinvestment in US commercial capability; how much of 180 Medical's cost base is fixed, and how its operating margin would respond to the Medicare competitive bidding price change from Jan 2028; what depreciation increase follows from the 2025 to 2027 growth capital expenditure programme (growth capital expenditure of $121m in 2025 and a guided $135m to $165m a year in 2026 and 2027). Focus: How much of the guided move from a 22.3 percent adjusted operating margin in FY2025 to 24 to 26 percent by 2027 rests on fixed-cost leverage versus remaining automation and shared-services savings, and how reliably the H2 2026 margin step (described by Bernstein as an all-time high H2 margin skew) can be delivered given the company's historical H1-to-H2 cost behaviour.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Coloplast (closest chronic-care peer; guides neutral-to-negative pricing in its 3 Nov 2026 framework); Hollister (private; US ostomy leader and GPO incumbent); Smith & Nephew (wound peer exposed to the same US skin-substitute reset); B. Braun (private ostomy and continence competitor in Europe); BD (external female catheters, a continence alternative); Wellspect, part of Dentsply Sirona (intermittent catheter competitor); Medline (US distributor that also sells private-label urology and wound products); Organogenesis and MiMedx (skin-substitute makers, the precedent of a flat-rate Medicare reset); Insulet (tubeless patch-pump maker whose integrated cannula substitutes for infusion sets). Operating conditions: US Medicare fee-schedule pricing and the DMEPOS competitive bidding round for ostomy and continence supplies scheduled from 1 Jan 2028, with bidder registration from 1 Dec 2026 and a bipartisan bill of 9 Oct 2026 proposing a five-year exclusion; US hospital group-purchasing tenders (Vizient, Premier, HealthTrust); European national tariff and pricing reforms (France and Germany from Jan 2027, as disclosed by Coloplast); a period of input-cost inflation as 12-month raw-material hedges roll off in 2027; flat-rate Medicare payment for skin substitutes from 1 Jan 2026; launches of premium, evidence-backed products (UK Drug Tariff listing of ConvaNiox at £40 per dressing; new Medicare codes for hydrophilic catheters from Jan 2026); currency swings between the dollar, euro, sterling and Danish krone. Inputs: Like-for-like price history in ostomy, continence and advanced wound care from 2015 to 2026 for Convatec and peers, and what Convatec's Strategic Pricing Centre of Excellence has delivered (the company reported a 30 basis point pricing benefit in FY2025 and H1 2026 against a history of price erosion); how much of category revenue growth comes from mix (soft convex ostomy systems, hydrophilic catheters, silicone foams) rather than price, and the price premiums these products carry; the pricing terms of Convatec's infusion-set supply contracts with pump makers (Medtronic and MiniMed, Tandem) and drug partners (AbbVie, Supernus, Tanabe), including any indexation, volume commitments and renewal dates; what prior DMEPOS competitive bidding rounds in other categories (for example diabetic testing supplies, CPAP and mobility) did to supplier prices, to manufacturers' prices, to the number of suppliers and to the volume share of winners, and over what period; what share of the expected retail price cut in ostomy and continence would pass to manufacturers (Bernstein modelled a 30 percent retail and 15 percent wholesale cut, 4 Jun 2026) and how 180 Medical, as a vertically integrated supplier, would be affected; the legislative status and likelihood of the 9 Oct 2026 bill; the details, timing and affected categories of the France and Germany pricing measures from Jan 2027; the size and trend of group-purchasing price concessions (Convatec reported a 27 basis point gross-margin effect in 2025) and the terms of the Vizient and Premier ostomy contracts won in Nov 2025 and Feb 2026; how Convatec's and peers' prices and margins behaved, and with what lag, during the 2022 to 2023 inflation spike; what customers, distributors and expert sources say about the durability of premium pricing for ConvaNiox, Esteem Body and GentleCath Air. Focus: Whether Convatec can hold or raise net price anywhere outside launch mix, and how large, how certain and how well-dated the 2027 European and 2028 US Medicare price events are for its products and for its 180 Medical channel.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The case rests on loops in which hospital wins, launches, contracted Infusion capacity and the consolidation of US home-delivery suppliers compound into future volume, set against a balancing loop in which fast-growing reimbursed categories attract payer price resets and competitor reinvestment. Peers: Coloplast (the incumbent most likely to respond to share loss); Hollister (US ostomy leader and GPO incumbent); Smith & Nephew (wound peer hit by the same payer reset); Medline (distributor and private-label competitor); Insulet (patch-pump maker that hired Convatec's former head of research in Jun 2026); AbbVie (Vyalev, the subcutaneous Parkinson's therapy that uses Convatec's sets); Organogenesis (skin-substitute maker, the payer-reset precedent). Inputs: The hospital-discharge loop: how US ostomy GPO access (Captis/Vizient from Nov 2025; Premier AscenDrive from Feb 2026; the HealthTrust contract held by Hollister expiring 30 Sep 2026) converts into acute placements and then into community patients who stay with the brand for years, with dated precedents of share recovery after GPO re-entry and how long it took; the launch loop: whether product launches (Esteem Body, ConvaFoam, GentleCath Air for Women, Natura Body in 2027, ConvaNiox) build clinical evidence, nurse adoption and share that fund further R&D, and the evidence that it is running now; the Infusion loop: how adoption of insulin pumps and subcutaneous Parkinson's therapies drives set demand, how set specification in partners' regulatory filings locks in volume, and how capacity constraints have limited it (a new automated line is due in 2027); the competitive-bidding loop: whether supplier consolidation from over 3,000 to a handful of national contract holders hands volume to 180 Medical and lets it switch patients to own-brand products, against the price cut; the balancing loops: whether payer cost containment follows category growth (the skin-substitute reset of 2026 and DMEPOS bidding), whether Coloplast's reinvestment of most of its DKK 500m savings into US commercial capability responds to Convatec's share gains, and whether group-purchasing concessions grow as volume grows; the automation loop: whether savings are reinvested in launches or retained in margin. Focus: Which of these loops are measurably running now and how long they ran in precedents, especially whether US ostomy GPO re-entry converts into community share fast enough to support the company's 6 to 8 percent organic growth target from 2027, and whether the payer balancing loop is likely to reach ostomy and continence beyond the 2028 bidding round.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Earnings per share over the next four years depend heavily on per-share and below-operating-profit inputs (buybacks, interest, tax, the amortisation roll-off, currency translation and capital expenditure) that are driven by stated capital-allocation policy and currency, where management statements and broker models can differ. Peers: Smith & Nephew (buyback-led EPS growth at a similar leverage level); Coloplast (leverage, dividend policy and currency translation); Siemens Healthineers (currency, tariff and refinancing effects on EPS); Philips (adjusting items and cash conversion). Inputs: Share buybacks after the $200m programme due to complete by 31 Dec 2026: management's stated policy (net debt to adjusted EBITDA of about 2.0 times; Jefferies reported no ambition to go below it; Morgan Stanley described buybacks as the default use of excess cash) against the share counts in published broker models for FY2027 to FY2029; net interest: the debt structure (the $500m 5.30 percent senior notes due 2035; the revolving credit facility and its currency mix), the floating-rate share and the weighted average borrowing rate (5.2 percent in FY2025); tax: the adjusted book rate (24 percent in FY2025), the gap to the cash rate and any expected changes; the end of amortisation of the 2008 spin-out intangibles in Dec 2026 (about $93m to $95m a year) and its effect on reported EPS and on brokers' bases; depreciation from the 2025 to 2027 growth capital expenditure and the expected normalisation of capital expenditure after the guided $200m to $230m in FY2026; adjusting items (restructuring; impairments of $72m in FY2025 and $69m in H1 2026) and whether they recur; currency: the sensitivity of dollar-reported EPS and of the sterling share price to GBP/USD, the transaction exposures to the euro, Danish krone and Mexican peso, and the 12-month hedging horizon; the dividend payout policy of 35 to 45 percent; the published broker estimates of the EPS effect of competitive bidding in 2028 (Morgan Stanley, Oct 2026: a 3 to 8 percent cut to 2028 consensus EPS if it proceeds, mid-single-digit upside if the 9 Oct 2026 bill passes). Focus: How large the buyback, interest, tax, depreciation and currency contributions to EPS are likely to be in FY2027 to FY2029 under management's stated policies, and how far published broker models diverge from those policies.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 17 November 2026, 20:11 to 20:26 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Convatec (CTEC-GB): second set of questions, translating the foundations into the operating and financial model

As at 17 Nov 2026. Written for the Investment Analyst who answers the third and fourth sets (predictability of the forecasts; what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

  • Everything here is written before the ten-month trading update of 18 Nov 2026. That update is the first hard test of the base case below. The two numbers to check are (i) organic growth excluding InnovaMatrix for the ten months (the base case needs about 5.5 to 5.8 percent year to date, which implies about 6.2 to 6.5 percent for Jul to Dec), and (ii) any wording on the FY2026 adjusted operating margin "at least 23.0 percent". If the update says "upper half" or "above" on growth, raise FY2026 to FY2027 revenue by about 0.5 percent; if it softens the margin language, take FY2026 to about 22.5 percent and FY2027 to about 23.3 percent.
  • Basis. All figures are in US dollars on the company's adjusted basis, which is the basis consensus uses (brokers'-basis FY2025 EPS 0.176 USD equals the company's adjusted diluted 17.6 cents; brokers'-basis FY2025 EBITDA $646m, 26.5 percent). "Adjusted operating margin" is the company's adjusted EBIT margin (22.3 percent in FY2025, $544m). "EBITDA" is adjusted EBIT plus depreciation and amortisation excluding amortisation of acquired intangibles (about $102m in FY2025). Never compare with FactSet's history lines (FactSet EBIT 16.6 percent in FY2025 is after amortisation).
  • The convention behind the base case. Where the company's record says the modal outcome is "delivered at the floor" but the distribution has a fatter downside tail (the FY2026 margin, the FY2027 margin), the inputs are set about 10 to 20 basis points below the modal outcome, so they are close to the probability-weighted value. Where the outcome is binary (competitive bidding in 2028), the base case is "it proceeds", sized at the low end of management's range, which is close to the probability-weighted midpoint of that range.
  • The base case in one line (USD): revenue 2,550 / 2,712 / 2,875 / 3,070 / 3,279m for FY2026 to FY2030 (+4.6, +6.4, +6.0, +6.8, +6.8 percent); adjusted operating margin 22.9 / 23.8 / 24.1 / 24.6 / 25.0 percent; EBITDA margin 27.2 / 28.3 / 28.8 / 29.3 / 29.6 percent; adjusted EPS 19.8 / 22.7 / 25.2 / 28.6 / 32.5 cents (+12.5, +14.6, +11.0, +13.6, +13.5 percent); free cash flow after interest, tax, leases and all capital expenditure about 233 / 292 / 362 / 412 / 451m. Against consensus (20.0 / 23.0 / 26.0 / 29.1 cents): -1 percent, -1 percent, -3 percent, -2 percent.
  • Where I disagree with the first set (each explained in the relevant answer): 1. The InnovaMatrix "base effect" in FY2027 is optical, not additive (C-20, C-67). InnovaMatrix revenue falls from $69m (FY2025) to about $7.5m (FY2026) and about $3m (FY2027). The drag on group growth is about -2.5 points in FY2026 and only about -0.2 points in FY2027. So group organic growth "rises" by about 3 points from FY2026 to FY2027, but FY2027 reported growth is simply ex-InnovaMatrix organic growth plus currency. Consensus FY2027 revenue growth of +6.8 percent therefore needs ex-InnovaMatrix organic growth of about 7 percent at flat currency, the midpoint of the 6 to 8 percent target. It is not "helped by the InnovaMatrix base", as the first set's C-67 table says; it is a full-strength midpoint assumption. 2. Interest-rate sensitivity is much smaller than the first set's C-76 says. The company's own disclosure (Annual Report 2025) is that 100 basis points on rates moves annual interest by about $2m (after $75m of swaps and with $500m fixed at 5.30 percent to 2035), not the first set's "$10m". It rises to perhaps $4m to $6m as the revolving facility is drawn to fund buybacks, still under 1 percent of EPS. 3. The legacy amortisation ended in July 2026, not December 2026 (C-20, C-54). The company confirmed in Aug 2026 that the 2008 spin-out intangibles were fully amortised in Jul 2026; H1 2026 carried the last $48m. Reported EPS steps up already in H2 2026 and fully in FY2027. 4. Consensus does not merely "omit buybacks after 2026" (C-19, C-67); it is internally consistent with deleveraging. Consensus net interest falls from about $75m (FY2026) to about $59m (FY2028), which implies net debt falling to roughly 1.3 times EBITDA. The flat share count and the falling interest are two halves of one assumption that contradicts the stated 2.0 times policy. Restoring the policy adds shares bought back and adds interest; the net EPS gain is about 1.5 percent a year, not the 2 percent the first set used and far below Morgan Stanley's gross "10 percent EPS tailwind". 5. Buyback capacity in FY2027 is about $225m to $250m on a full cash definition, not $250m to $350m (C-19). The first set used FactSet's free cash flow, which does not deduct intangible capital expenditure, lease payments or the cash cost of adjusting items, and assumed the current low cash tax rate persists. Capacity reaches $325m to $375m only by FY2029 to FY2030.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports revenue by franchise (Advanced Wound Care, Ostomy Care, Continence Care including 180 Medical, Infusion Care) but reports adjusted operating profit only for the group, so segment margins are not given. Qualitatively: Infusion is margin-accretive (contracted volume on automated lines); 180 Medical earns a retail margin of about 25 percent gross and 10 to 15 percent EBIT on top of the 60 to 65 percent manufacturing gross margin, which is why competitive bidding hurts margin more than revenue; Advanced Wound Care lost about 140 basis points of group margin in H1 2026 to InnovaMatrix, a drag that has now gone.

Revenue, USDm (reported growth) FY2025 actual FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 FactSet consensus FY2026 / FY2027 / FY2028
Infusion Care 473 523 (+10.6%) 581 (+11.0%) 642 (+10.5%) about 703 (+9.5%) about 766 (+9.0%) 520 / 576 / 631
Ostomy Care 676 723 (+7.0%) 765 (+5.8%) 810 (+5.9%, after about -$5m of bidding) about 860 (+6.2%) about 913 (+6.2%) 722 / 764 / 808
Continence Care (incl. 180 Medical) 537 573 (+6.7%) 605 (+5.6%) 617 (+2.0%, after about -$24m of bidding) about 651 (+5.5%) about 689 (+5.8%) 574 / 611 / 644
Advanced Wound Care (incl. InnovaMatrix) 753 (InnovaMatrix 69) 731 (-2.9%; InnovaMatrix about 7.5) 761 (+4.1%; InnovaMatrix about 3) 806 (+5.9%) about 858 (+6.5%) about 914 (+6.5%) 739 / 788 / 840
Group 2,439 2,550 (+4.6%) 2,712 (+6.4%) 2,875 (+6.0%) 3,070 (+6.8%) 3,279 (+6.8%) 2,553 / 2,728 / 2,913

FY2029 to FY2030 segment figures are approximate and sum to within about $5m of the group line. The main segment difference from consensus is Advanced Wound Care: ex-InnovaMatrix organic growth was 3.4 percent in H1 2026, and consensus needs a sharp H2 acceleration plus about 7 percent in FY2027; the base case has about 3.6 percent organic in FY2026 and 4.8 percent in FY2027 (Europe price cuts from Jan 2027; ConvaNiox immaterial before 2028).

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Market growth about 4.5 percent; ex-InnovaMatrix organic growth 5.7 percent (FY2026; implies about 6.4 percent in H2 after 5.0 percent in H1), 6.5 percent (FY2027), 7.2 percent before and about 6.1 percent after competitive bidding (FY2028), 6.8 percent (FY2029 to FY2030). Drivers: Infusion at about 10 to 11 percent (contracted orders, Parkinson's sets for AbbVie, Supernus and Tanabe, a new automated high-speed line in 2027); Wave 1 and Wave 2 launches (Esteem Body, Natura Body US from Q2 2027, GentleCath Air for Women and male, ConvaFoam, ConvaFiber, ConvaVac; ConvaNiox from 2028); slow conversion of the Vizient and Premier ostomy contracts. InnovaMatrix $69m to about $7.5m to about $3m to zero: about -2.5 points of group growth in FY2026 and only about -0.2 points in FY2027. Currency about +1.5 points in FY2026 (GBP/USD about 1.34), zero thereafter. Like-for-like price +0.3 (FY2026), -0.1 (FY2027, France and Germany), about -1.5 in FY2028 (bidding, of which about +0.5 points comes back as volume at 180 Medical, so net revenue -1.0 percent), +0.2 (FY2029 to FY2030). Mix +1 to 1.5 points a year inside volume.
  • Half-years. H2 2026 revenue about $1,318m (+4.7 percent); H1 2027 about $1,311m (+6.4 percent, the first half without the InnovaMatrix drag); H2 2027 about $1,401m (+6.3 percent).
  • Margins. Adjusted operating margin 22.9 / 23.8 / 24.1 / 24.6 / 25.0 percent. H2 2026 about 24.4 percent (H1 21.2 percent) on flat operating costs, seasonal volume, Infusion mix and productivity (management's bridge about +370 basis points against about +340 needed). FY2027: hedge roll-off costs about 70 basis points of gross margin (each point of inflation above 3 percent is $7m to $8m), offset by automation completion (Slovakia, Mexico, Dominican Republic), Hyderabad shared services and the absence of InnovaMatrix costs in H1; operating expenses grow about 4 percent. FY2028: competitive bidding about -75 basis points gross, about -50 net after own-brand switching and cost action. Adjusted gross margin about 60.0 percent in FY2026 to FY2027, rising about 20 to 40 basis points a year from FY2028. The EBITDA margin is the adjusted operating margin plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles (about 4.3 to 4.7 percent of sales, rising with growth capital expenditure towards about $150m).
  • Below the line. Net financial items at about 5.3 percent all-in on average net debt excluding leases, which is held near 2.0 times company adjusted EBITDA (about $1,470m at end-2026 rising to about $1,930m at end-2030). Adjusted tax rate 23.0 percent in FY2026 (H1 audit release), 24.0 percent thereafter; cash tax assumed to rise from about 20 to 24 percent by FY2028 (conservative). No minorities. Share-plan issuance about 6m shares a year.
  • Cash. Capital expenditure peaks in FY2026 (guided $200m to $230m; growth $135m to $165m in 2026 and 2027) and normalises to about 5.6 to 6.0 percent of sales from FY2028 (guided 5 to 7 percent). Net working capital (FactSet definition: receivables plus inventories less payables) rises from 10.2 percent of revenue at end-2025 to 10.6 percent at end-2026 (inventory built for the H2 acceleration and new capacity), then drifts down. Lease payments about $24m to $28m a year (FactSet lease liabilities about $110m). Acquisitions: none in FY2026; about $20m a year of small bolt-ons thereafter. Buybacks: the $200m programme plus $22m paid in H1 2026; then about $225m, $275m, $325m and $350m, sized to roughly 90 to 100 percent of capacity under the 2.0 times policy (free cash flow after dividends plus the debt increase that keeps leverage at 2.0 times). Buyback prices assumed about $3.50, $3.80, $4.10 and $4.40 a share for FY2027 to FY2030. Dividend per share progressive at about 38 to 41 percent of adjusted EPS (policy 35 to 45 percent).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Consensus is on the company's adjusted basis. FactSet's brokers'-basis actuals for FY2025 (revenue $2,439m, EBITDA $646m, EPS 0.176 USD) match the company's adjusted figures (adjusted diluted EPS 17.6 cents). These inputs are on the same basis.
  • Adjusted operating profit (company) = reported operating profit + amortisation of acquired intangibles + impairments + restructuring and other adjusting items. FY2025: adjusted operating profit $544m (22.3 percent); adjusting items $228m, of which $72m Triad impairment and about $120m amortisation of acquired intangibles (the 2008 spin-out intangibles about $93m to $95m, ended Jul 2026 with $48m in H1 2026; about $20m to $25m a year of other acquired amortisation continues); only $12m was cash. H1 2026 added a $69m InnovaMatrix impairment (including a $21m receivables provision).
  • Adjusted EBITDA (brokers' basis) = adjusted operating profit + depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles (FY2025: 544 + about 102 = 646, 26.5 percent). The company's own leverage EBITDA runs about 3 percent higher (net debt $1,330m at 2.0 times). FactSet's operating EBITDA history ($630m in FY2025) and FactSet's EBIT history ($406m, 16.6 percent, after amortisation) are not comparable and must not be used as a base.
  • Adjusted net income = adjusted operating profit less net finance costs less tax at the adjusted rate (24 percent in FY2025); reported net income was $175m in FY2025 (reported basic EPS 8.6 cents) against adjusted about $360m. From FY2027 reported EPS should be about 21 cents against adjusted 22.7 cents, as the legacy amortisation has ended.
  • Free cash flow. The company's "free cash flow to equity" ($362m in FY2025) excludes growth capital expenditure; on the legacy definition it was $219m. FactSet's free cash flow (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital expenditure: $195m in FY2025; consensus $230m, $338m, $403m) excludes intangible capital expenditure. The cross-check below deducts all capital expenditure (including intangibles), lease payments, interest, tax and the cash cost of adjusting items, before dividends, buybacks and acquisitions, which is closest to the legacy free cash flow to equity.
  • Currency. Figures are in US dollars; the shares trade in pence. At GBP/USD 1.34, 19.8 cents is about 14.8 pence and 22.7 cents about 16.9 pence; each 1 percent rise in GBP/USD cuts pence EPS about 1 percent on translation.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Convatec Group plc (CTEC-GB), a London-listed, US-dollar-reporting maker of chronic-care medical consumables in four segments: Advanced Wound Care (Aquacel Hydrofiber, ConvaFoam, ConvaNiox, ConvaVac; formerly the InnovaMatrix skin substitute), Ostomy Care (Esteem Body, Natura Body), Continence Care (GentleCath catheters and the US home-delivery distributor 180 Medical) and Infusion Care (infusion sets for insulin pumps and subcutaneous Parkinson's therapies)

Request: T-D1

Peers: Coloplast (closest listed chronic-care peer; reset its 2030 ambition on 3 Nov 2026 and is reinvesting most of its Funding the Journey savings in US Chronic Care); Hollister (private, employee-owned US ostomy leader and GPO incumbent); Smith & Nephew (listed wound-care peer whose cost programmes and margin rebuild are a precedent for self-help); Mölnlycke (private, owned by Investor AB; wound-care peer with the iMpact30+ efficiency programme); Ypsomed (listed high-volume drug-delivery consumables maker with automated lines and contracted volumes, the closest model for Convatec's Infusion capacity build); Insulet (tubeless patch-pump maker with an integrated cannula, a substitute for infusion sets, which hired Convatec's former research chief in Jun 2026) Operating conditions: the step-up in second-half operating margin required to meet a full-year floor; input-cost inflation as 12-month raw-material hedges roll off (2027); a year of leadership transition (new chief executive since Nov 2025, new chief financial officer from 1 Mar 2027, research and global operations leadership changes in 2026); a dense product-launch programme under a newly centralised organisation; a competitor that has chosen to reinvest savings into the same US accounts; a large contracted manufacturing-capacity ramp in 2027 Inputs: Has Convatec, or any comparable chronic-care or medtech consumables company, delivered a sequential second-half adjusted operating margin step of about 300 to 350 basis points over the first half (Bernstein, 26 Jun 2026, called Convatec's FY2026 requirement an "all-time high" second-half skew), and what distinguished the cases that delivered from those that missed? How did Convatec's adjusted operating margin and operating expenses actually behave between H1 and H2 in each year from 2019 to 2025? Can Convatec lift ex-InnovaMatrix organic growth from 5.0 percent (H1 2026) to the 6 to 8 percent targeted from 2027 on its demonstrated launch record (Esteem Body, ConvaFoam, GentleCath Air for Women), and what is the evidence on Wave 2 launches (Natura Body, GentleCath Air male, ConvaFiber, ConvaVac) since the research chief left in Jun 2026? In the 2022 to 2023 inflation period, how did Convatec (FY2022 margin 19.5 percent against a floor above 19.7 percent) and peers protect margins, and what does that record imply for 2027? How have companies fared that delivered a margin target during a chief financial officer and operations-leadership handover? How long did Coloplast's 2020 to 2021 Vizient and Premier wins take to show in its US community share, and what does that imply for Convatec's Nov 2025 and Feb 2026 wins? Can the new automated Infusion line be commissioned and validated on time, given Convatec's own statement that automated lines take up to three years to build and validate? Focus: Whether the H2 2026 margin step and the 2027 move to at least 24 percent rest on strengths Convatec has already shown (overhead discipline, automation, Infusion contracts) or on strengths it would need to acquire (faster launch conversion, stronger US acute share, pricing), and how often comparable companies have achieved similar steps.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Coloplast (most comparable revenue mix and payer exposure); Smith & Nephew (wound-care peer whose forecasts have repeatedly been cut); Solventum (wound-care leader with separation costs and margin dilution); Mölnlycke (private wound-care peer with disclosed margins); AdaptHealth and Viemed (US durable-medical-equipment distributors exposed to competitive bidding, the closest read-across to 180 Medical); Organogenesis and MiMedx (skin-substitute makers, the dated precedent of a sudden Medicare payment reset) Operating conditions: US Medicare DMEPOS competitive bidding for ostomy and urological supplies from 1 Jan 2028 (bidder registration from 1 Dec 2026; bidding window 5 Jan to 10 Mar 2027; results expected late summer 2027) with a bipartisan delay bill introduced on 9 Oct 2026; France and Germany chronic-care and wound pricing measures from Jan 2027; polymer and freight inflation after hedge roll-off; currency moves between the dollar, sterling, euro and Danish krone; leverage held at about 2.0 times with buybacks as the default use of surplus cash Inputs: Which drivers separate Convatec's next 18 months to four years from FY2019 to FY2025: Infusion growth at about double digits (FY2025 organic 12.5 percent; H1 2026 7.4 percent on order phasing), the launch programme, the end of the InnovaMatrix drag, operating-expense leverage, the 2027 hedge roll-off, competitive bidding, European price cuts and post-2026 buybacks; how concentrated are they? How dispersed have outcomes been for European medtech consumables companies whose forecasts depended on a second-half margin acceleration or on a single regulatory decision? What did prior DMEPOS bidding rounds (CPAP, diabetic testing supplies, mobility) do to supplier prices, manufacturer prices, the number of suppliers and winners' volume, and over what period? How wide were the outcomes for Coloplast in 2019 when France cut ostomy and continence prices by about 9 percent, and for Coloplast and Convatec after the Sep 2024 French CNAM cut? How much has a one-point change in Convatec's organic growth, a 50 basis point change in margin and a 10 percent move in GBP/USD moved its EPS in practice? What do published broker bull and bear cases (Morgan Stanley 7 Sep 2026; Jefferies 23 Jul 2026; DNB Carnegie 22 Oct 2026) assume, and how wide is the spread of broker FY2027 and FY2028 EPS forecasts? Focus: Whether the forecast difference against the last six years rests on a few drivers (such as Infusion volume, the H2 2026 to 2027 overhead and automation bridge, and the buyback policy) and on one binary decision (competitive bidding) or on many, and how wide and how skewed the 18-month range of outcomes is as a result.
